§0   Orientation: conditioning as arithmetic, processes as families

Sources: L12 slides 1–8 · L13 slides 1–8 · L14 slides 1, 3–8 · L15 slides 1–4, 6 · Bertsekas–Tsitsiklis (cited throughout as B&T) §4.3, §4.5, Chapter 6 preamble, §6.1–6.2 · numbers from computes/g4_s0.py. A bare "§N.k" always means this note's own subsection; every reference to the textbook carries the "B&T" prefix.

Notes G1–G3 built the machinery: a probability model and its axioms (G1 §1), conditioning and Bayes' rule (G1 §2), independence (G1 §3), random variables described by a PMF (G2) or a density (G3), together with expectation, variance, joint and conditional distributions, and derived distributions. Everything so far has concerned one experiment observed through finitely many random variables, and conditioning has been used mostly as a way of slicing a model — restricting attention to a smaller universe.

This note (G4, lectures L12–L15, B&T §4.3, §4.5 and Chapter 6) makes two moves, and they are worth naming before any formula appears, because everything in §1–§5 is one of the two.

  • Theme A — conditioning becomes a computational tool, not just a modeling device. The object $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ is promoted from a table of numbers to a single random variable. That one reinterpretation converts two hard quantities, $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)$, into easy ones, via the law of iterated expectations and the law of total variance (L12 slides 2–3).
  • Theme B — random processes. A model is allowed to contain an infinite family of random variables indexed by time. We study exactly two such families in this note, both of "arrival" type: the Bernoulli process in discrete time (L13, B&T §6.1) and the Poisson process in continuous time (L14–L15, B&T §6.2). They are the same process seen at two time resolutions, and §0.2 sets up the dictionary between them.

The two themes are not independent: the single most useful application of Theme A, the random sum $Y=X_1+\cdots+X_N$ with a random number $N$ of terms (L12 slides 7–8), is exactly what you need when $N$ counts the arrivals of a process. That is why the two themes share a note.

0.1 Theme A — the conceptual jump: $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ is a random variable

Nothing in the definition is new. For a fixed value $y$ of a random variable $Y$ with $\mathbf{P}(Y=y)>0$, G2 §3 already defined the conditional expectation of $X$ given the event $\{Y=y\}$, $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]=\sum_x x\,p_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y),$$ with the integral $\int x f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)\,dx$ in the continuous case (L12 slide 2; here $x$ ranges over the values of $X$, and $p_{X\mid Y}(\cdot\mid y)$ is the conditional PMF of G2 §4). For each fixed $y$ this is a number.

The jump is to stop fixing $y$. Define the ordinary function $$g(y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y],$$ which assigns a number to each possible value $y$ of $Y$ — a deterministic function, computable once and for all from the model. Now feed the random input $Y$ into it. The composition $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\;:=\;g(Y)$$ is a function of a random variable, hence (G2 §1) a random variable in its own right: when the experiment produces the outcome that makes $Y=y$, this new random variable takes the value $g(y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$. It therefore has its own PMF, its own expectation $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]$, and its own variance $\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)$ — and those two objects are precisely the content of §1. The same construction applied to the conditional variance gives a second random variable $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)$, "with value $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)$ when $Y=y$" (L12 slide 3).

Gotcha — capital versus lowercase. $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$ and $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ differ by one character and are different kinds of object: the first is a number (one per $y$), the second is a random variable (a function of $Y$). Consequently $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]\big]$ is meaningless — you cannot average a constant over nothing — while $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]$ is the law of iterated expectations. L12 slides 7–8 lean on exactly this distinction (see the transcript's own footnote: "this capital/lowercase distinction is load-bearing"). Two further points: $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ is a function of $Y$ only — $X$ has been averaged away, so writing something like "$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=X/2$" is a type error; and $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ is not in general a constant, even though its expectation is the constant $\mathbb{E}[X]$.

A first look at what it buys you

L12 slides 4–5 make the payoff concrete with a class split into two sections. Section $y=1$ has $n_1=10$ students whose quiz scores average $90$; section $y=2$ has $n_2=20$ students averaging $60$. The model the slides leave implicit (a [DERIVATION GAP] flagged in the transcript) is this: pick one student uniformly at random out of the $n_1+n_2=30$, let $X$ be that student's score and $Y$ that student's section. Then $Y$ has PMF $$\mathbf{P}(Y=1)=\frac{10}{30}=\frac13,\qquad \mathbf{P}(Y=2)=\frac{20}{30}=\frac23 .$$ Conditioning on $\{Y=1\}$ means the chosen student is one of the $10$ in section 1, each now equally likely with probability $1/10$, so $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=1]=90$; likewise $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=2]=60$. As a random variable, $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\begin{cases}90,&\text{with probability } 1/3,\\ 60,&\text{with probability } 2/3,\end{cases}$$ a genuine two-valued random variable with a PMF of its own. Averaging it gives $$\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\tfrac13(90)+\tfrac23(60)=30+40=70,$$ and the direct computation over all $30$ students gives $\mathbb{E}[X]=\frac{90\cdot 10+60\cdot 20}{30}=\frac{900+1200}{30}=\frac{2100}{30}=70$ — the same number. That agreement is the law of iterated expectations, and it is the whole trick: the class average was recovered without ever touching an individual score, from two section averages and two weights.

The variance version is more striking still. With the within-section score variances $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=1)=10$ and $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=2)=20$ given on L12 slide 5, $$\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]=\tfrac13(10)+\tfrac23(20)=\tfrac{10}{3}+\tfrac{40}{3}=\frac{50}{3}\approx 16.6667,$$ $$\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)=\tfrac13(90-70)^2+\tfrac23(60-70)^2=\tfrac13(400)+\tfrac23(100)=\tfrac{400}{3}+\tfrac{200}{3}=\frac{600}{3}=200,$$ and the law of total variance adds them: $$\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac{50}{3}+200=\frac{650}{3}\approx 216.6667 .$$ So $7.69\%$ of the spread in quiz scores is variability within sections and $92.31\%$ is variability between them (all values from computes/g4_s0.py). §1 derives both laws in full, including the two suppressed steps in the L12 slide 3 proof; §1 also solves the unfinished two-piece-density example of L12 slide 6.

Interpretation. Read the law of total variance as an analysis of variance: total spread = average spread within groups + spread between group means (L12 slide 5 writes exactly this in words). It is the reason $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ deserves to be a random variable — you want to ask how much it varies, and only random variables have variances. It is also why $Y$ is worth observing at all: $\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])$ measures how much knowing $Y$ moves your forecast of $X$, and $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]$ measures the uncertainty that observing $Y$ cannot remove. B&T §4.3 (Example 4.19, "forecast revisions") makes the same point: the expected revision to a forecast, $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]-\mathbb{E}[X]\big]$, is exactly $0$ — you cannot predict today which way tomorrow's news will push you.

0.2 Theme B — a random process is an infinite family of random variables

B&T open Chapter 6 by insisting that this is not a conceptual departure: "a stochastic process is simply a (finite or infinite) sequence of random variables"; all of them "refer to a single and common experiment, and are therefore defined on a common sample space." The experiment is still one experiment; there are just infinitely many numbers to read off its outcome. What changes is the emphasis (B&T Chapter 6 preamble): one asks about dependencies across time, about long-run averages over the whole sequence, and about the likelihood of rare boundary events — questions that only make sense once the family is infinite.

L13 slide 3 puts this as two views of the same object, and the tension between them is instructive.

  • First view — a sequence of random variables $X_1,X_2,\ldots$ For the Bernoulli process each $X_t$ is an independent Bernoulli trial with $\mathbf{P}(X_t=1)=p$, so (filling the slide's blanks, a [DERIVATION GAP]) $\mathbb{E}[X_t]=p$ and $\operatorname{var}(X_t)=p(1-p)$, straight from G2 §2–§3. This view is enough for any question about finitely many time slots.
  • Second view — what is the right sample space? L13 slide 3 asks it and answers with a warning shot: $\mathbf{P}(X_t=1\text{ for all }t)$. On the first view you would compute $\mathbf{P}(X_1=\cdots=X_n=1)=p^n$ by independence, and then note that this holds for every $n$; since $0<p<1$, letting $n\to\infty$ gives $\lim_{n\to\infty}p^n=0$. The natural sample space is therefore the set of all infinite binary sequences — an uncountable set, on which individual outcomes have probability $0$ exactly as in G3 §0 (the wheel of fortune). Infinitely many trials is a genuinely bigger model than any finite one.

L13 slide 3 also names the three processes of the course, and the split is the map of the rest of the syllabus:

  • Bernoulli process — memoryless, discrete time (this note, §2);
  • Poisson process — memoryless, continuous time (this note, §3–§4);
  • Markov chains — with memory/dependence across time (note G5; previewed in §5).

The first two are the arrival-type processes of B&T §6.1–6.2, characterized by independent interarrival times: geometric in discrete time, exponential in continuous time. The third relaxes independence in the mildest possible way ("the next value depends on past values only through the current value", B&T Chapter 6 preamble), and is the whole of B&T Chapter 7.

The comparison table

Almost every fact about the Poisson process has a Bernoulli twin, and knowing which is which saves memorizing two lists. Table 0.1 is L14 slide 7's comparison, completed with the moment and mean rows the slide leaves for the lectures, and with the section of this note where each entry is derived. Every named distribution in the table was already built in G2 (binomial, geometric, Pascal, Poisson) or G3 (exponential, Erlang) — the processes contribute the context, not the formulas.

FeatureBERNOULLI process (§2)POISSON process (§3–§4)
Times of arrivaldiscrete: slots $t=1,2,\ldots$continuous: any $t\ge 0$
Arrival rate$p$ per trial$\lambda$ per unit time
Defining assumptionindependent trials, $\mathbf{P}(X_t=1)=p$time homogeneity + independence across disjoint intervals + $P(1,\delta)\approx\lambda\delta$
PMF of number of arrivalsbinomial: $\binom{n}{k}p^k(1-p)^{n-k}$ in $n$ slotsPoisson: $P(k,\tau)=\dfrac{(\lambda\tau)^k e^{-\lambda\tau}}{k!}$ in duration $\tau$
Mean / variance of that count$np$  /  $np(1-p)$$\lambda\tau$  /  $\lambda\tau$ (equal!)
Interarrival-time distributiongeometric: $(1-p)^{t-1}p$, mean $1/p$ slotsexponential: $\lambda e^{-\lambda t}$, mean $1/\lambda$
Time to the $k$th arrivalPascal: $\binom{t-1}{k-1}p^k(1-p)^{t-k}$, mean $k/p$Erlang: $\dfrac{\lambda^k y^{k-1}e^{-\lambda y}}{(k-1)!}$, mean $k/\lambda$
Memorylessnessyes — the future after any time is a fresh Bernoulli processyes — the time to the next arrival is independent of the past
Merging / splittingyes (merged: collisions count as one arrival)yes (merged rate $\lambda_1+\lambda_2$)
Table 0.1 — The Bernoulli/Poisson correspondence, reproducing L14 slide 7 and completing it from L13 slides 4–6, L14 slides 3–6 and L15 slide 2; the merging/splitting row is from L13 slides 7–8 (Bernoulli splitting and merging, collisions counted as one arrival), L14 slide 8 and L15 slides 4 and 6 (Poisson merging at rate $\lambda_1+\lambda_2$, splitting into rates $p\lambda$ and $(1-p)\lambda$). B&T §6.1–6.2.

The table is not a coincidence: the right-hand column is the $\delta\to 0$ limit of the left. L14 slide 7 draws the discretization explicitly — chop $[0,t]$ into $n=t/\delta$ slots of width $\delta$ and declare a slot "successful" with probability $p=\lambda\delta$, so that the expected number of arrivals is $$np=\frac{t}{\delta}\cdot\lambda\delta=\lambda t,$$ independent of $\delta$, as the slide's third annotation states. Two numerical checks, both from computes/g4_s0.py, show the dictionary working (§3 does the limit properly).

  • Counts. Take $\lambda=5$ messages per hour and $t=1/2$ hour, so $\lambda t=2.5$ (L14 slide 5). The Poisson answers are $P(0,\tau)=e^{-2.5}=0.082085$ and $P(1,\tau)=2.5e^{-2.5}=0.205212$. The binomial approximations with $p=2.5/n$ give, for $k=0$: $0.031250$ at $n=5$, $0.071790$ at $n=25$, $0.081059$ at $n=250$, $0.081982$ at $n=2500$ — errors $0.050835$, $0.010295$, $0.001026$, $0.000103$, shrinking by a factor of about $10$ each time $n$ grows tenfold. For $k=1$: $0.156250$, $0.199416$, $0.204693$, $0.205161$, converging to $0.205212$. The variance converges too: the binomial variance $np(1-p)$ is $1.25$ at $n=5$ but $2.475$ at $n=250$, approaching the Poisson value $\lambda t=2.5$, since the factor $(1-p)=1-\lambda t/n\to 1$.
  • Interarrival times. Take $\lambda=0.6$ catches per hour (the L15 slide 3 fishing rate) and one-minute slots, $\delta=1/60$ hour, so $p=\lambda\delta=0.01$. The geometric mean is $1/p=100$ slots, which in hours is $\delta/p=(1/60)/0.01=1.666667$ — exactly $1/\lambda=1/0.6=1.666667$. The variance matches only in the limit: $\delta^2(1-p)/p^2=2.75$ hour$^2$ against the exponential $1/\lambda^2=2.777778$, a gap of $1\%$ that closes as $\delta\to0$ (the discrepancy is the factor $1-p$).
Gotcha — three things the table hides.
  • Two rate symbols, two units. $p$ is a dimensionless probability per slot and must satisfy $p\le 1$; $\lambda$ is a rate with units 1/time and may be any positive number. "$\lambda=5$" is not "a probability of 5"; only the product $\lambda\delta$ over a small interval is a probability, and only for $\delta$ small enough that $\lambda\delta\le1$.
  • Poisson's mean equals its variance ($\mathbb{E}[N_\tau]=\operatorname{var}(N_\tau)=\lambda\tau$, L15 slide 2) — a peculiarity with no analogue on the Bernoulli side, where $np(1-p)<np$ strictly. It is the surviving trace of the vanishing factor $1-p$.
  • The geometric counts slots, the exponential measures time. The geometric interarrival time takes values $1,2,3,\ldots$ (never $0$: at least one trial must occur), while the exponential takes values in $[0,\infty)$ with $\mathbf{P}(T=0)=0$. Comparing the two means requires multiplying by the slot width $\delta$, as in the check above; comparing $1/p$ with $1/\lambda$ directly is a units error.

0.3 Roadmap

Fig. 0.1 — The map of note G4: two themes, one bridge, one shared engine. Theme A is L12 slides 2–3; Theme B is L13 slide 3 and L14 slide 7; the random-sum bridge is L12 slides 7–8; the terminus is the third process named on L13 slide 3.

The five substantive sections, with the lectures they follow:

  • §1 — Conditional expectation as a random variable; iterated expectations; total variance (L12 slides 2–6, B&T §4.3). The construction of §0.1 done properly: $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ and $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)$ as random variables, the law of iterated expectations $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\mathbb{E}[X]$ with proof, the law of total variance with every step of the L12 slide 3 argument restored, the stick-breaking example ($\mathbb{E}[X]=\ell/4$), the quiz-score example in full, and the unfinished two-piece-density example of L12 slide 6 solved. Hook: the fastest way to compute an expectation is often to condition on something you do not care about.
  • §2 — Random sums and the Bernoulli process (L12 slides 7–8, L13 slides 2–8, B&T §4.5, §6.1). $Y=X_1+\cdots+X_N$ with $N$ random and independent of the i.i.d. $X_i$: $\mathbb{E}[Y]=\mathbb{E}[N]\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $\operatorname{var}(Y)=\mathbb{E}[N]\operatorname{var}(X)+(\mathbb{E}[X])^2\operatorname{var}(N)$, both by the two laws of §1. Then the Bernoulli process: binomial counts, geometric interarrivals and their memorylessness, the Pascal PMF for the $k$th arrival, and merging and splitting (L13 slides 7–8). Hook: the variance formula has two terms because two different things are random — how much each term contributes, and how many terms there are.
  • §3 — The Poisson process I (L14, B&T §6.2). The three defining properties (time homogeneity, independence over disjoint intervals, small-interval probabilities), the derivation of the Poisson PMF as the $\delta\to0$ limit of the binomial, $\mathbb{E}[N_t]=\operatorname{var}(N_t)=\lambda t$, and the exponential and Erlang interarrival laws. Hook: one small-interval assumption, $P(1,\delta)\approx\lambda\delta$, generates the entire table above.
  • §4 — The Poisson process II: merging, splitting, and random incidence (L15, B&T §6.2). Merging independent Poisson processes into rate $\lambda_1+\lambda_2$; which stream the next arrival belongs to; splitting by independent coin flips; the Erlang distribution in use; and random incidence — the paradox that an interval you land in at a random moment is longer, on average, than a typical interval. Hook: the bus that is always late is not late; you are sampling with a bias (compare the G2 §2 bus-size example).
  • §5 — Synthesis and the bridge to Markov chains (all four lectures, B&T Chapter 6 summary, Chapter 7 preview). A consolidated tool-selection pass, the common failure modes of §1–§4, mixed problems, and the step that G5 takes: dropping independence across time in favor of dependence through the current state only. Hook: Bernoulli and Poisson are the two processes with no memory at all; everything after them is about remembering exactly one thing.

Every recitation problem of rec12–rec15 is worked in full inside a collapsible solution box, but each one is placed in the section that owns its topic, which is not always the section with the matching number. The map is: rec12 — invariance of the correlation coefficient, the Laplace density of a difference of two exponentials, polar coordinates of a normal pair (Rayleigh radius, uniform angle) and the Schwarz inequality — is the largest recitation block of the note and is worked in §1.5 (Examples 1.11–1.14), since it consolidates the G3 material that §1 builds on; rec13 — generalized iterated expectations, breaking a stick twice, and widgets in boxes as a random sum — in §2.1 and §2.2 (Examples 2.1, 2.2, 2.4); rec14 — mosquito and tick bites as merged Bernoulli processes, Al's Pascal-and-random-sum problem, and the coupon collector — is pure Bernoulli-process and random-sum material owned by §2, but it is worked in §3.5 (Examples 3.7–3.9), where the Bernoulli/Poisson dictionary is assembled and the same problems can be read twice, once per process; and rec15 — type-A/type-B light bulbs, the service station, and $\mathbf{P}(X<Y<Z)$ for three exponentials — in §4.6 (Examples 4.5–4.7).

Behind you: G1 (models, conditioning, Bayes, independence), G2 (PMFs, expectation, variance, joint PMFs, the geometric and Poisson PMFs) and G3 (densities, the exponential and its memorylessness, joint and conditional densities, convolution) — all used constantly and not restated. Ahead: G5 builds Markov chains on the third bullet of L13 slide 3, and G6 returns to $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)$ when it treats estimation.

§0 in one screen. Theme A. $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$ is a number; $g(y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$ is a function; $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=g(Y)$ is a random variable (L12 slide 2). So is $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)$. Averaging them gives the two workhorses of §1: $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]+\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])$ — within-group spread plus between-group spread. Quiz-score check: $\tfrac13(90)+\tfrac23(60)=70=\mathbb{E}[X]$, and $\operatorname{var}(X)=\tfrac{50}{3}+200=\tfrac{650}{3}\approx216.6667$, of which $92.31\%$ is between-section. Theme B. A random process is an infinite family of random variables on one sample space (B&T Chapter 6). Bernoulli = discrete time, rate $p$; Poisson = continuous time, rate $\lambda$; Markov chains = memory (G5). Bernoulli $\to$ Poisson under $\delta\to0$ with $p=\lambda\delta$, $n=t/\delta$, $np=\lambda t$ (L14 slide 7): binomial $\to$ Poisson ($0.081982$ vs $0.082085$ at $n=2500$, $\lambda t=2.5$), geometric $\to$ exponential ($\delta/p=1.666667=1/\lambda$), Pascal $\to$ Erlang. Route: §1 the two laws → §2 random sums + Bernoulli process → §3 Poisson process I → §4 Poisson II: merging, splitting, random incidence → §5 synthesis and the bridge to Markov chains.

§1   Conditional expectation as a random variable

Sources: L12 slides 1–6 (raster p01–p02) · rec12 problems 1–4 (+ official solutions) · Bertsekas–Tsitsiklis §4.2 (covariance and correlation, pp. 217–222), §4.3 (conditional expectation and variance revisited, Examples 4.16–4.21, pp. 222–228), §4.1 (derived distributions), B&T Problems 4.16 (p. 248) and 4.20 (p. 250). Every number on this page is recomputed in computes/g4_s1.py; every figure is generated by computes/g4_s1_figs.py.

G2 §3 and G3 §2 taught you to condition and then average: fix the value $Y=y$, work inside the smaller universe where that is known, get a number $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$, then weight those numbers by $p_Y(y)$ or $f_Y(y)$ and add. That is the total expectation theorem, and it is a computational device. This section takes one small step that turns the device into a way of thinking: instead of treating $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$ as a table of numbers indexed by $y$, we treat the whole table as a single random variable $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ — the one whose value is $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$ on the outcomes where $Y$ happens to equal $y$.

The payoff is two formulas that will carry the rest of this note. The law of iterated expectations, $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\mathbb{E}[X]$, is the total expectation theorem rewritten so that it composes: you can condition on anything, in any order, and the averages agree. The law of total variance, $\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]+\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)$, has no counterpart at all in the earlier chapters: it splits randomness into within-group and between-group parts and is the tool that makes random sums (§2), Bernoulli and Poisson process quantities (§§2–4), and estimation errors (G6) computable. L12's stick-breaking and class-sections examples are done here in full, the lecture's unsolved section-quiz example is solved, and recitation 12 — which consolidates the correlation and derived-distribution machinery of G3 — is worked end to end.

Interpretation. Everything below is one sentence made precise: knowing $Y$ changes your best guess about $X$, and the amount by which it changes is itself random. The law of iterated expectations says the change averages out to nothing. The law of total variance says the change nevertheless accounts for a definite share of the total uncertainty in $X$ — the share you would remove by learning $Y$.

1.1 From a number to a random variable: $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$ versus $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$

Definition (conditional expectation given a value; L12 slide 2, B&T §4.3). Let $X$ and $Y$ be random variables on the same experiment, and let $y$ be a value with $p_Y(y)>0$ (discrete case) or $f_Y(y)>0$ (continuous case). The conditional expectation of $X$ given $Y=y$ is $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]\;=\;\sum_x x\,p_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)\quad\text{(discrete)},\qquad \mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]\;=\;\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} x\,f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)\,dx\quad\text{(continuous)} .$$ Here $p_{X\mid Y}(\cdot\mid y)$ and $f_{X\mid Y}(\cdot\mid y)$ are the conditional PMF and PDF of G2 §4 and G3 §2 — legitimate probability laws in their own right — and $x$ ranges over the values $X$ can take. For each fixed $y$ this is a number.

Now vary $y$. Every value of $y$ produces one number, so we have defined a function $$g(y)\;=\;\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y],$$ a perfectly ordinary real-valued function of a real variable. Feed it the random variable $Y$ instead of a number $y$ and you get a new random variable, by the same "function of a random variable is a random variable" rule from G2 §1.

Definition (conditional expectation as a random variable; L12 slide 2, B&T §4.3). $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ denotes the random variable $g(Y)$, where $g(y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$. On any outcome of the experiment for which $Y$ takes the value $y$, the random variable $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ takes the value $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$. Its distribution is inherited from the distribution of $Y$.
Gotcha — the one piece of notation everything here depends on.
$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$a number. It depends on $y$, which is a bound symbol you supply, not on chance. You can plot it against $y$; you cannot ask for its variance.
$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$a random variable. It is a function of $Y$, so it has a distribution, an expectation, and a variance. It is not a function of $X$ — $X$ has been averaged away.
$\mathbb{E}[X]$a number, the unconditional mean.
Three symptoms of getting this wrong: writing "$\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y])$" (the variance of a number, always $0$); writing "$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\sum_y \mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]p_Y(y)$" (that is $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]$, a number, not the random variable); and treating $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ as still depending on $X$ — for instance "cancelling" $X$ against it.
Fig. 1.1 — $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ is the function $g$ evaluated at the random argument $Y$: the randomness of $Y$ (horizontal) is pushed through $g$ to become randomness of $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ (vertical). Left: discrete $Y$ (L12 slide 4). Right: continuous $Y$ (L12 slide 2; B&T Example 4.17).
Example 1.1 (two class sections; L12 slide 4, B&T Example 4.18). A class of $30$ students is split into two sections: section $1$ has $10$ students whose quiz scores average $90$, section $2$ has $20$ students whose scores average $60$. Pick one student uniformly at random; let $X$ be that student's score and $Y\in\{1,2\}$ the student's section. Find $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$, the random variable $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ and its distribution, and $\mathbb{E}[X]$.

The probability model (the slide does not spell this out — [DERIVATION GAP] filled). "Pick a student uniformly at random" means each of the $30$ students has probability $1/30$. The event $\{Y=1\}$ contains the $10$ students of section 1, so $$\mathbf{P}(Y=1)=\frac{10}{30}=\frac13,\qquad \mathbf{P}(Y=2)=\frac{20}{30}=\frac23 .$$ That is where the $1/3$ and $2/3$ on the slide come from — they are section sizes, not anything about scores.

Conditional expectations. Given $\{Y=1\}$, the chosen student is uniform among the $10$ students of section 1, so each has conditional probability $1/10$, and $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=1]=\sum_{i=1}^{10}x_i\cdot\frac1{10}=\frac1{10}\sum_{i=1}^{10}x_i=90,$$ which is precisely the section-1 average given on the slide. Identically $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=2]=\frac1{20}\sum_{i=11}^{30}x_i=60$. So $g(1)=90$ and $g(2)=60$.

The random variable. $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=g(Y)$ takes the value $90$ exactly when $Y=1$ and $60$ exactly when $Y=2$: $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\begin{cases}90,&\text{with probability }1/3,\\[2pt] 60,&\text{with probability }2/3.\end{cases}$$ It is a two-valued random variable — a coarse-grained version of $X$ in which every student has been replaced by his or her section average.

Its mean. By the expected value rule applied to this two-valued random variable, $$\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\tfrac13\cdot 90+\tfrac23\cdot 60=30+40=70 .$$ And directly, averaging all $30$ scores, $\mathbb{E}[X]=\frac1{30}\sum_{i=1}^{30}x_i=\frac{90\cdot10+60\cdot20}{30}=\frac{900+1200}{30}=\frac{2100}{30}=70$ (sec_EX). The two agree — that is the law of iterated expectations, proved in §1.2.

Example 1.2 (stick breaking; L12 slide 2, B&T Example 4.17). A stick of length $\ell$ is broken at a point chosen uniformly over its length; keep the left piece, of length $Y$. Break that piece again at a point chosen uniformly over its length; keep the left piece, of length $X$. Find $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$, then $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$.

Solution — filling the [DERIVATION GAP] on the slide. The slide simply asserts $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]=y/2$. Here is why. Condition on $\{Y=y\}$ for some $0<y\le\ell$. In that universe the second break point is, by the statement of the experiment, uniformly distributed over the interval $[0,y]$, and $X$ is that break point. So the conditional density is the uniform density on $[0,y]$ (G3 §1): $$f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)=\frac1y,\qquad 0\le x\le y,$$ and $0$ otherwise. Apply the definition: $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x\,f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)\,dx=\int_0^{y}x\cdot\frac1y\,dx =\frac1y\left[\frac{x^2}{2}\right]_0^{y}=\frac1y\cdot\frac{y^2}{2}=\frac{y}{2},$$ using only the antiderivative of $x$ and the fact that $1/y$ is constant in $x$. (You could also quote the mean of a $U(0,y)$ variable from G3 §1, which is the midpoint $(0+y)/2$.) Hence $g(y)=y/2$.

The random variable. Replace the dummy $y$ by the random $Y$: $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\frac{Y}{2}.$$ Since $Y\sim U(0,\ell)$, the random variable $Y/2$ is uniform on $(0,\ell/2)$ — the right panel of Fig. 1.1 is exactly this statement: the horizontal uniform density is squeezed by the factor $1/2$ into the vertical one.

Fig. 1.2 — The two-break experiment and the resulting density of $X$, $f_X(x)=\ln(\ell/x)/\ell$ (drawn with $\ell=1$). The mean $\ell/4$ and variance $7\ell^2/144\approx0.048611\,\ell^2$ are derived in §§1.2 and 1.4 (L12 slide 2; B&T Example 4.17).
Example 1.3 (a coin whose bias is random; B&T Example 4.16). Because of manufacturing defects the probability of heads of a coin is itself a random variable $Y$, uniform on $[0,1]$. The coin is tossed $n$ times independently; $X$ is the number of heads. Find $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$.

Solution. Condition on $\{Y=y\}$. In that universe the tosses are $n$ independent Bernoulli trials with common success probability $y$, so $X$ is binomial with parameters $n$ and $y$ (G2 §1), whose mean is $ny$ (G2 §2). Thus $g(y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]=ny$ and $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=nY .$$ Note the shape of the answer: a random variable built out of $Y$ alone, with $X$ gone. With $n=10$ this is $10Y$, uniform on $[0,10]$.

Property (two rules you will use constantly; B&T §4.3).
  1. Pulling out what is known. For any function $h$, $\;\mathbb{E}\big[X\,h(Y)\,\big|\,Y\big]=h(Y)\,\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$. Reason: inside the universe $\{Y=y\}$ the quantity $h(y)$ is a constant, and constants come out of expectations (G2 §2).
  2. Independence collapses it. If $X$ and $Y$ are independent then $p_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)=p_X(x)$ for every $y$ (G2 §4), so $g(y)=\mathbb{E}[X]$ for every $y$ and therefore $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\mathbb{E}[X]$ — a constant random variable. Conditioning on something irrelevant changes nothing.
  3. Linearity survives. $\mathbb{E}[aX_1+bX_2\mid Y]=a\,\mathbb{E}[X_1\mid Y]+b\,\mathbb{E}[X_2\mid Y]$, because linearity holds inside each conditional universe and the identity therefore holds value by value of $y$.
Gotcha — $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ is a function of $Y$, and only of $Y$. Whatever $X$ was, it has been integrated out. Two consequences that trip people up: (i) $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\,\big|\,Y\big]=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$, since conditioning a function of $Y$ on $Y$ does nothing; (ii) $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid Y]=Y$, not $\mathbb{E}[Y]$. If your answer for $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ still contains the symbol $X$, you have not finished the computation.
Practice 1.1. Roll a fair six-sided die; let $X$ be the outcome and let $Y=1$ if $X$ is even and $Y=0$ if $X$ is odd. Write down $g(y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$, the distribution of $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$, and verify $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\mathbb{E}[X]$.
Solution

Given $\{Y=0\}$ the outcome is uniform on $\{1,3,5\}$, each with conditional probability $1/3$, so $$g(0)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=0]=\tfrac13(1+3+5)=\tfrac13\cdot9=3 .$$ Given $\{Y=1\}$ the outcome is uniform on $\{2,4,6\}$, so $g(1)=\tfrac13(2+4+6)=\tfrac13\cdot12=4$ (pr1_EX_odd, pr1_EX_even).

Since $\mathbf{P}(Y=0)=\mathbf{P}(Y=1)=3/6=1/2$, $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\begin{cases}3,&\text{w.p. }1/2,\\ 4,&\text{w.p. }1/2,\end{cases}\qquad \mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\tfrac12\cdot3+\tfrac12\cdot4=\tfrac72=3.5,$$ which equals $\mathbb{E}[X]=\frac{1+2+3+4+5+6}{6}=\frac{21}{6}=3.5$ ✓ (pr1_EX).

Practice 1.2. Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent with $\mathbb{E}[X]=\mu$. Simplify (a) $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$, (b) $\mathbb{E}[XY\mid Y]$, (c) $\mathbb{E}[XY]$, and (d) $\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)$.
Solution

(a) By rule 2 above, independence gives $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\mathbb{E}[X]=\mu$, a constant.

(b) Take $h(Y)=Y$ in rule 1: $\mathbb{E}[XY\mid Y]=Y\,\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=Y\mu=\mu Y$.

(c) Average (b): $\mathbb{E}[XY]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[XY\mid Y]\big]=\mathbb{E}[\mu Y]=\mu\,\mathbb{E}[Y]=\mathbb{E}[X]\mathbb{E}[Y]$ — the familiar product rule for independent variables (G2 §4), now obtained by conditioning.

(d) A constant random variable has variance $0$, so $\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)=0$. Anticipating §1.3: when $Y$ says nothing about $X$, the "between-group" part of the variance vanishes and $\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]$.

1.2 The law of iterated expectations

Theorem (law of iterated expectations; L12 slide 2, B&T §4.3). For any random variables $X$ and $Y$ on the same experiment, provided $\mathbb{E}[X]$ is well defined and finite, $$\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]\;=\;\mathbb{E}[X].$$ The inner expectation is taken over $X$ with $Y$ held fixed; the outer one is taken over $Y$.

Proof, discrete case, every step. Write $g(y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$, so that $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=g(Y)$. Apply the expected value rule (G2 §2) to the function $g$ of the random variable $Y$: $$\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\mathbb{E}[g(Y)]=\sum_y g(y)\,p_Y(y) =\sum_y \Big(\sum_x x\,p_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)\Big)p_Y(y),$$ where the last equality substitutes the definition of $g$. Move $p_Y(y)$ inside the inner sum (it does not depend on $x$) and use the multiplication rule $p_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)\,p_Y(y)=p_{X,Y}(x,y)$ from G2 §4: $$=\sum_y\sum_x x\,p_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)p_Y(y)=\sum_y\sum_x x\,p_{X,Y}(x,y).$$ Both sums are over countably many terms and (under the stated finiteness) absolutely convergent, so they may be exchanged: $$=\sum_x x\sum_y p_{X,Y}(x,y)=\sum_x x\,p_X(x)=\mathbb{E}[X],$$ the middle step being the marginalization identity $\sum_y p_{X,Y}(x,y)=p_X(x)$ (G2 §4) and the last the definition of $\mathbb{E}[X]$. $\blacksquare$

Proof, continuous case. Identical with sums replaced by integrals and PMFs by PDFs: $$\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} g(y)f_Y(y)\,dy =\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} x\,f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)f_Y(y)\,dx\,dy =\int\!\!\int x\,f_{X,Y}(x,y)\,dx\,dy=\int x f_X(x)\,dx=\mathbb{E}[X],$$ using $f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)f_Y(y)=f_{X,Y}(x,y)$ (G3 §2), Fubini's theorem to exchange the order of integration, and $\int f_{X,Y}(x,y)\,dy=f_X(x)$. The mixed case ($Y$ continuous, $X$ discrete, or vice versa) goes the same way with the corresponding Bayes-variation formulas of G3 §3. $\blacksquare$

Interpretation — three readings of the same equation.
  1. Divide and conquer. To find $\mathbb{E}[X]$, split the experiment into cases according to $Y$, compute the mean in each case, and average the case means with the case probabilities as weights. This is the total expectation theorem of G2 §3, and the theorem says the packaging as a random variable loses nothing.
  2. Weighted averaging of subgroup averages. In Example 1.1, the class average is the section averages weighted by section sizes — exactly $70=\frac13\cdot90+\frac23\cdot60$. The weights must be the sizes; the unweighted average $\frac{90+60}{2}=75$ is a different (and wrong) number.
  3. Forecast revisions have mean zero (B&T Example 4.19). If $\mathbb{E}[X]$ is a January forecast of annual sales $X$ and $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ is the revised forecast made in July after first-semester sales $Y$ are known, then the revision is $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]-\mathbb{E}[X]$, and $$\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]-\mathbb{E}[X]\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]-\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}[X]-\mathbb{E}[X]=0 .$$ You cannot forecast your own future forecast revision. If you expected the revision to be positive, your January forecast was too low to begin with.
Example 1.4 (the stick's mean, completed; L12 slide 2 — [DERIVATION GAP] filled). Continue Example 1.2 and compute $\mathbb{E}[X]$.

Solution. The slide writes $\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}[Y/2]=\ell/4$ without showing $\mathbb{E}[Y]$. Since the first break point is uniform on $[0,\ell]$, $Y\sim U(0,\ell)$ and (G3 §1, or by direct integration) $$\mathbb{E}[Y]=\int_0^{\ell}y\cdot\frac1\ell\,dy=\frac1\ell\left[\frac{y^2}{2}\right]_0^{\ell}=\frac1\ell\cdot\frac{\ell^2}{2}=\frac{\ell}{2}.$$ Then by the law of iterated expectations and linearity, $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{Y}{2}\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[Y]}{2}=\frac{\ell/2}{2}=\frac{\ell}{4}.$$ Check without conditioning. The marginal density of $X$ (obtained in G3 §2 for this experiment) is $f_X(x)=\frac1\ell\ln(\ell/x)$ for $0<x<\ell$; with $\ell=1$, numerical integration gives $\int_0^1\ln(1/x)\,dx=1.000000$ (it is a density ✓) and $\int_0^1 x\ln(1/x)\,dx=0.250000=\ell/4$ ✓ (stick_fX_check, stick_EX_quad). Four million simulated experiments give $0.249845$ (stick_EX_mc). The conditioning route needed one line; the direct route needed the whole density.

Example 1.5 (the coin with random bias, mean; B&T Example 4.16). Continue Example 1.3: $Y\sim U(0,1)$, $X\mid\{Y=y\}\sim\text{Bin}(n,y)$.

Solution. $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=nY$, and $\mathbb{E}[Y]=\frac{0+1}{2}=\frac12$, so $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}[nY]=n\,\mathbb{E}[Y]=\frac{n}{2}.$$ With $n=10$ that is $\mathbb{E}[X]=5$ (coin_EX); two million simulated experiments give $4.999161$ (coin_EX_mc). Sensible: a coin of unknown bias, symmetric about $1/2$, behaves on average like a fair one.

Gotcha — average the right thing. $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]=\mathbb{E}[X]$ requires the outer average to be over the distribution of $Y$. Two frequent errors: (i) averaging the values $g(y)$ with equal weights instead of weights $p_Y(y)$ — in Example 1.1 that gives $75$ instead of $70$; (ii) using $\mathbb{E}[g(Y)]=g(\mathbb{E}[Y])$. That last one is legal only when $g$ is affine. It is fine for the stick ($g(y)=y/2$, affine), which is why $\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}[Y]/2$ works; it is not fine in general, and §1.3 is essentially the study of the gap $\mathbb{E}[g(Y)]\ne g(\mathbb{E}[Y])$ for $g(y)=y^2$.
Practice 1.3. Let $N$ be uniform on $\{1,2,3,4\}$, and given $\{N=n\}$ let $X$ be uniform on $\{1,2,\dots,n\}$. Find $\mathbb{E}[X]$.
Solution

Inside the universe $\{N=n\}$, $X$ is uniform on $n$ consecutive integers starting at $1$, whose mean is the midpoint $\frac{1+n}{2}$. So $g(n)=\frac{n+1}{2}$ and $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid N]=\frac{N+1}{2}.$$ Now $\mathbb{E}[N]=\frac{1+2+3+4}{4}=\frac{10}{4}=2.5$ (pr2_EN), so by the law of iterated expectations and linearity $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{N+1}{2}\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N]+1}{2}=\frac{2.5+1}{2}=\frac{3.5}{2}=1.75 .$$ Check by brute force. Building the joint PMF $p_{N,X}(n,x)=\frac14\cdot\frac1n$ for $1\le x\le n$ and summing gives total probability $1.000000$ and $\mathbb{E}[X]=1.750000$ ✓ (pr2_EX_brute).

1.3 Conditional variance and the law of total variance

The same "make it a random variable" move applies to the variance. Recall from G2 §3 that inside the conditional universe $\{Y=y\}$ every notion of probability has a conditional counterpart; in particular the variance of $X$ in that universe is the expected squared deviation from that universe's own mean.

Definition (conditional variance; L12 slide 3, B&T §4.3). $$\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)\;=\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(X-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]\big)^2\;\Big|\;Y=y\Big],$$ a number for each $y$. Writing $h(y)=\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)$, the random variable $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y):=h(Y)$ takes the value $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)$ whenever $Y=y$. Note the deviation is measured from the conditional mean $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$, not from $\mathbb{E}[X]$.
Theorem (law of total variance; L12 slide 3, B&T §4.3). $$\operatorname{var}(X)\;=\;\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]}_{\text{average variability }\textbf{within}\text{ groups}}\;+\;\underbrace{\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)}_{\text{variability }\textbf{between}\text{ groups}} .$$

Proof, following L12 slide 3 with the two missing steps supplied ([DERIVATION GAP] filled). Four lines, labelled as on the slide.

(a) The variance identity from G2 §3, valid for any random variable $W$ with finite second moment: $\operatorname{var}(W)=\mathbb{E}[W^2]-(\mathbb{E}[W])^2$.

(b) Apply (a) inside the universe $\{Y=y\}$ — everything there is an honest probability law, so the identity holds verbatim: $$\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)=\mathbb{E}[X^2\mid Y=y]-\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]\big)^2 .$$ This is an identity between numbers, true for every $y$; replacing $y$ by $Y$ makes it an identity between random variables, $$\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)=\mathbb{E}[X^2\mid Y]-\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)^2 .$$

(c) Take the expectation of (b) and use linearity of expectation: $$\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}[X^2\mid Y]\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)^2\Big].$$ The first term is where the slide skips a step: apply the law of iterated expectations of §1.2 to the random variable $X^2$ in place of $X$, which is legitimate because that law holds for any random variable with a finite mean. Hence $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X^2\mid Y]\big]=\mathbb{E}[X^2]$ and $$\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)^2\Big].$$

(d) Now apply (a) to the random variable $W=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ — legitimate precisely because §1.1 established that it is a random variable: $$\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)^2\Big]-\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big]\Big)^2 =\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)^2\Big]-\big(\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2,$$ the second equality being the law of iterated expectations again (this is the second step the slide leaves implicit).

Add (c) and (d). The term $\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])^2\big]$ appears once with a minus sign and once with a plus sign, so it cancels: $$\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]+\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-\big(\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2=\operatorname{var}(X)$$ by (a) applied to $X$ itself. $\blacksquare$

Interpretation — decomposing uncertainty (L12 slide 5). Think of $Y$ as sorting the population into groups.
  • $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)$ measures how spread out $X$ is inside group $y$. Averaging over groups, $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]$ is the within-group (or unexplained, or residual) variance — the uncertainty that would remain even after you were told which group you are in.
  • $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$ is the level of group $y$. Its variance $\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])$ is the between-group (or explained) variance — the uncertainty that comes purely from not knowing which group you are in.
  • Total uncertainty is exactly their sum. Both pieces are variances, hence $\ge 0$, so $\operatorname{var}(X)\ge\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)\ge\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])$: conditioning can never increase the average variance. Observing $Y$ removes the between-group share and nothing more.
This is the probabilistic form of the analysis of variance; the ratio $\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])/\operatorname{var}(X)$ is the fraction of the variance "explained" by $Y$.
Example 1.6 (two class sections, variance; L12 slides 4–5). Continue Example 1.1. Suppose in addition that the within-section mean squared deviations are $$\frac1{10}\sum_{i=1}^{10}(x_i-90)^2=10,\qquad \frac1{20}\sum_{i=11}^{30}(x_i-60)^2=20 .$$ Find $\operatorname{var}(X)$.

Step 1 — the conditional variances. Given $\{Y=1\}$ the student is uniform among the $10$ students of section 1 whose mean is $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=1]=90$, so by definition $$\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=1)=\sum_{i=1}^{10}\frac1{10}(x_i-90)^2=10,$$ and likewise $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=2)=20$. The displayed sums are the conditional variances; that is why the data were given in that form.

Step 2 — the random variable $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)$ and its mean. $$\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)=\begin{cases}10,&\text{w.p. }1/3,\\20,&\text{w.p. }2/3,\end{cases}\qquad \mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]=\tfrac13\cdot10+\tfrac23\cdot20=\tfrac{10}{3}+\tfrac{40}{3}=\frac{50}{3}\approx16.666667$$ (sec_Evar). Bigger section 2 is noisier and gets the larger weight, so the average sits close to $20$.

Step 3 — the variance of $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$. From Example 1.1, $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ equals $90$ w.p. $1/3$ and $60$ w.p. $2/3$, with mean $70$. By the definition of variance, $$\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)=\tfrac13(90-70)^2+\tfrac23(60-70)^2=\tfrac13\cdot400+\tfrac23\cdot100=\frac{400}{3}+\frac{200}{3}=\frac{600}{3}=200$$ (sec_varE). Cross-check with the $\mathbb{E}[W^2]-(\mathbb{E}[W])^2$ form: $\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])^2\big]=\tfrac13\cdot8100+\tfrac23\cdot3600=2700+2400=5100$ and $5100-70^2=5100-4900=200$ ✓ (sec_E_condmean_sq).

Step 4 — add. $$\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac{50}{3}+200=\frac{50+600}{3}=\frac{650}{3}\approx216.666667$$ (sec_varX). Building an explicit $30$-score population with exactly these section means and variances and computing its variance directly gives $216.666667$ ✓ (sec_pop_var).

Reading the answer. Only $\frac{50/3}{650/3}=\frac{50}{650}\approx0.076923$, i.e. $7.7\%$, of the score variance is within sections; $92.3\%$ is between them (sec_within_share, sec_between_share). Knowing a student's section tells you almost everything about the student's score in this class.

Fig. 1.3 — The law of total variance for Example 1.6: within-group variance $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]=50/3\approx16.67$ plus between-group variance $\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])=200$ equals $\operatorname{var}(X)=650/3\approx216.67$ (L12 slides 4–5).
Gotcha — four ways to garble the law.
  1. The two terms are not interchangeable. $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]$ takes the expectation of a variance; $\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])$ takes the variance of an expectation. In Example 1.6 they are $16.67$ and $200$ — swapping them is not a small error.
  2. Variances of the conditional distributions do not simply average to $\operatorname{var}(X)$. Writing $\operatorname{var}(X)=\sum_y \operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)p_Y(y)$ drops the between-group term entirely; it is correct only when all the group means coincide.
  3. There is no "law of total standard deviation". The decomposition is additive in variances, never in standard deviations: $\sqrt{216.67}=14.72\ne\sqrt{16.67}+\sqrt{200}=4.08+14.14=18.22$.
  4. $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)$ is measured about $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$, not about $\mathbb{E}[X]$. Using the grand mean inside the groups double-counts the between-group part and inflates the answer.
Recipe (computing means and variances by conditioning; L12 slides 2–3, B&T §4.3).
  1. Choose the conditioning variable $Y$. Pick the quantity whose value makes the description of $X$ simple — the stage-one outcome, the group label, the number of trials, the unknown parameter.
  2. Work inside the universe $\{Y=y\}$. Identify the conditional distribution of $X$ there, then read off $g(y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$ and $h(y)=\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)$ as formulas in $y$. Usually you recognize a named distribution and quote its mean and variance.
  3. Promote $y$ to $Y$: $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=g(Y)$ and $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)=h(Y)$. Both are functions of $Y$ alone; if $X$ still appears, redo step 2.
  4. Mean: $\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}[g(Y)]$, computed with the expected value rule over the distribution of $Y$.
  5. Variance: $\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[h(Y)]+\operatorname{var}(g(Y))$. Both terms need only moments of $Y$; note $\operatorname{var}(g(Y))$ typically requires $\mathbb{E}[Y^2]$, so compute $\mathbb{E}[Y]$ and $\mathbb{E}[Y^2]$ once and reuse them.
  6. Check: both terms are nonnegative, so $\operatorname{var}(X)$ must exceed each of them; and if $g$ is constant the between-group term must vanish.
Fig. 1.4 — Decision guide for the two laws. The left branch is the law of iterated expectations, the right branch the law of total variance (L12 slides 2–3).
Practice 1.4. Continue Practice 1.3 ($N$ uniform on $\{1,2,3,4\}$, $X\mid\{N=n\}$ uniform on $\{1,\dots,n\}$) and find $\operatorname{var}(X)$.
Solution

Step 2 of the recipe. A uniform distribution on $n$ consecutive integers has variance $\frac{n^2-1}{12}$ (G2 §3), so $h(n)=\frac{n^2-1}{12}$ and $\operatorname{var}(X\mid N)=\frac{N^2-1}{12}$.

Moments of $N$. $\mathbb{E}[N]=2.5$ and $\mathbb{E}[N^2]=\frac{1+4+9+16}{4}=\frac{30}{4}=7.5$, hence $\operatorname{var}(N)=7.5-2.5^2=7.5-6.25=1.25$ (pr2_EN2, pr2_varN).

Within. $\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid N)\big]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[N^2]-1}{12}=\frac{7.5-1}{12}=\frac{6.5}{12}=\frac{13}{24}\approx0.541667$ (pr2_Evar).

Between. $\displaystyle\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid N]\big)=\operatorname{var}\!\Big(\frac{N+1}{2}\Big)=\frac{\operatorname{var}(N)}{4}=\frac{1.25}{4}=0.3125=\frac{5}{16}$, using $\operatorname{var}(aN+b)=a^2\operatorname{var}(N)$ with $a=1/2$ (pr2_varE).

Total. $\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac{13}{24}+\frac{5}{16}=\frac{26}{48}+\frac{15}{48}=\frac{41}{48}\approx0.854167$. Brute-force summation over the joint PMF gives $0.854167$ ✓ (pr2_varX_brute).

Practice 1.5. A measurement $X$ comes from instrument 1 with probability $0.4$, in which case it is $N(0,1^2)$, and from instrument 2 with probability $0.6$, in which case it is $N(5,2^2)$. Decompose $\operatorname{var}(X)$ and state what fraction of it is explained by knowing the instrument.
Solution

Let $Y\in\{1,2\}$ be the instrument. Then $g(1)=0$, $g(2)=5$, $h(1)=1$, $h(2)=4$.

$\mathbb{E}[X]=0.4\cdot0+0.6\cdot5=3$ (pr3_EX).

Within: $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]=0.4\cdot1+0.6\cdot4=0.4+2.4=2.8$.

Between: $\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])=0.4(0-3)^2+0.6(5-3)^2=0.4\cdot9+0.6\cdot4=3.6+2.4=6$.

Total: $\operatorname{var}(X)=2.8+6=8.8$, so $\operatorname{sd}(X)=\sqrt{8.8}\approx2.966479$ (pr3_varX, pr3_sd). The explained fraction is $6/8.8\approx0.681818$, i.e. $68.2\%$ (pr3_between_share). Note that $X$ is not normal — a mixture of normals is bimodal here — yet the decomposition needs nothing beyond the two conditional means and variances.

1.4 The lecture's worked and unworked examples

Example 1.7 (L12 slide 6 — the [UNSOLVED EXAMPLE], solved in full). $X$ has the two-piece density $$f_X(x)=\begin{cases}1/3,&0\le x\le1,\\[2pt] 2/3,&1<x\le2,\\[2pt] 0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}$$ and $Y$ is the label of the piece: $Y=1$ on the event $\{0\le X\le1\}$ and $Y=2$ on $\{1<X\le2\}$. The slide leaves every right-hand side blank. Compute $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=1]$, $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=2]$, $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=1)$, $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=2)$, $\mathbb{E}[X]$, $\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])$, and verify the law of total variance.

Step 0 — is it a density? Total area $=\frac13\cdot1+\frac23\cdot1=\frac13+\frac23=1$ ✓ (q_area_check).

Step 1 — the distribution of $Y$. $\mathbf{P}(Y=1)=\mathbf{P}(0\le X\le1)=\int_0^1\frac13dx=\frac13$ and $\mathbf{P}(Y=2)=\int_1^2\frac23dx=\frac23$. (These are the two rectangle areas — the picture is doing the integration for you.)

Step 2 — the conditional densities. Conditioning a continuous random variable on an event of positive probability rescales the density on that event (G3 §2): $$f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid 1)=\frac{f_X(x)}{\mathbf{P}(Y=1)}=\frac{1/3}{1/3}=1,\quad 0\le x\le1;\qquad f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid 2)=\frac{2/3}{2/3}=1,\quad 1<x\le2 .$$ So given $Y=1$, $X\sim U(0,1)$; given $Y=2$, $X\sim U(1,2)$. Both conditionals are uniform on unit-length intervals — the two rectangles have different heights only because they carry different probabilities, not because their shapes differ.

Step 3 — conditional means and variances. For $U(a,b)$ the mean is the midpoint $(a+b)/2$ and the variance is $(b-a)^2/12$ (G3 §1): $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=1]=\frac{0+1}{2}=\frac12,\qquad \mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=2]=\frac{1+2}{2}=\frac32,$$ $$\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=1)=\frac{(1-0)^2}{12}=\frac1{12},\qquad \operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=2)=\frac{(2-1)^2}{12}=\frac1{12}.$$ (q_EX_Y1, q_EX_Y2, q_var_Y1, q_var_Y2.)

Step 4 — the unconditional mean. By the law of iterated expectations, $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\tfrac13\cdot\tfrac12+\tfrac23\cdot\tfrac32=\tfrac16+1=\frac{1+6}{6}=\frac76\approx1.166667$$ (q_EX). Direct integration confirms it: $\int_0^1 x\cdot\frac13dx+\int_1^2 x\cdot\frac23dx=\frac13\cdot\frac12+\frac23\cdot\frac32=\frac76$ ✓ (q_EX_direct).

Step 5 — the between-group term. $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]$ equals $\frac12$ w.p. $\frac13$ and $\frac32$ w.p. $\frac23$. Using $\mathbb{E}[W^2]-(\mathbb{E}[W])^2$: $$\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)^2\Big]=\tfrac13\cdot\tfrac14+\tfrac23\cdot\tfrac94=\tfrac1{12}+\tfrac32=\frac{1+18}{12}=\frac{19}{12},$$ $$\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)=\frac{19}{12}-\Big(\frac76\Big)^2=\frac{19}{12}-\frac{49}{36}=\frac{57-49}{36}=\frac{8}{36}=\frac29\approx0.222222 .$$ Deviation form as a cross-check: $\tfrac13(\tfrac12-\tfrac76)^2+\tfrac23(\tfrac32-\tfrac76)^2=\tfrac13\cdot\tfrac49+\tfrac23\cdot\tfrac19=\tfrac4{27}+\tfrac2{27}=\tfrac6{27}=\tfrac29$ ✓ (q_varE, q_varE_dev).

Step 6 — the within-group term and the total. Both conditional variances equal $1/12$, so the average is $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]=\tfrac13\cdot\tfrac1{12}+\tfrac23\cdot\tfrac1{12}=\tfrac1{12}\approx0.083333$, and $$\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac1{12}+\frac29=\frac{3}{36}+\frac{8}{36}=\frac{11}{36}\approx0.305556$$ (q_varX). Verification without conditioning: $\mathbb{E}[X^2]=\int_0^1\frac{x^2}{3}dx+\int_1^2\frac{2x^2}{3}dx=\frac13\cdot\frac13+\frac23\cdot\frac73=\frac19+\frac{14}{9}=\frac{15}{9}=\frac53$, so $\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac53-\big(\frac76\big)^2=\frac{60}{36}-\frac{49}{36}=\frac{11}{36}$ ✓ (q_EX2_direct, q_varX_direct).

Reading the answer. $\frac{2/9}{11/36}=\frac{8}{11}\approx0.727$: about $73\%$ of the variance is between the two pieces. The two conditional variances happened to be equal, which is why the within term is just $1/12$ regardless of the weights.

Fig. 1.5 — The slide-6 density with everything the slide left blank filled in: $\mathbb{E}[X]=7/6$, $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]=1/12$, $\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])=2/9$, $\operatorname{var}(X)=11/36$ (L12 slide 6).
Gotcha — two traps in Step 2 and Step 6 of Example 1.7.
  1. Conditioning on a positive-probability event rescales the density; it does not reshape it. The rule is $f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid 1)=f_X(x)/\mathbf{P}(Y=1)$ on the event, and $0$ off it — divide by the probability of the event, nothing else. The frequent error is to renormalize by the interval length, or to keep the original height $1/3$ and call it a conditional density (it integrates to $1/3$, not $1$), or to divide by $\mathbf{P}(Y=2)$ on the piece belonging to $Y=1$. Here the two rescalings, $\frac{1/3}{1/3}$ and $\frac{2/3}{2/3}$, both give height $1$ — a coincidence of this particular density, not a rule.
  2. Equal conditional variances are a coincidence of equal interval widths. In Example 1.7 both pieces have width $1$, so $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=1)=\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=2)=1/12$ and the weighted average $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)]$ comes out to $1/12$ whatever $\mathbf{P}(Y=1)$ is. Do not read that as "the weights don't matter": Example 1.8, immediately below, is the same problem with widths $1$ and $2$, where the conditional variances are $1/12$ and $1/3$ and the within term $\frac5{24}$ genuinely depends on the weights. Only $\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y])$ ever depends on the conditional means; the within term depends on the conditional spreads, and both are weighted by $p_Y$.
Example 1.8 (the textbook's variant; B&T Example 4.21). The book poses the same question with a different picture (its Figure 4.13): $f_X(x)=\frac12$ on $[0,1]$ and $f_X(x)=\frac14$ on $(1,3]$, with $Y=1$ and $Y=2$ labelling the two pieces.

Solution. Areas: $\mathbf{P}(Y=1)=\frac12\cdot1=\frac12$ and $\mathbf{P}(Y=2)=\frac14\cdot2=\frac12$ — here the two pieces are equally likely. Given $Y=1$, $X\sim U(0,1)$: mean $\frac12$, variance $\frac1{12}$. Given $Y=2$, $X\sim U(1,3)$: mean $\frac{1+3}{2}=2$, variance $\frac{(3-1)^2}{12}=\frac4{12}=\frac13$. Then $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac12+\tfrac12\cdot2=\tfrac14+1=\frac54 ,$$ $$\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)=\tfrac12\Big(\tfrac12-\tfrac54\Big)^2+\tfrac12\Big(2-\tfrac54\Big)^2=\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac9{16}+\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac9{16}=\frac9{16},$$ $$\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]=\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac1{12}+\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac4{12}=\frac{1}{24}+\frac{4}{24}=\frac5{24},$$ $$\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac5{24}+\frac9{16}=\frac{10}{48}+\frac{27}{48}=\frac{37}{48}\approx0.770833 ,$$ matching the book (bt_EX, bt_varE, bt_Evar, bt_varX). Contrast with Example 1.7: here the two conditional variances differ (one interval is twice as long), so the within term is a genuine weighted average.

Example 1.9 (the stick's variance; B&T Example 4.17 continued). Find $\operatorname{var}(X)$ for the two-break stick of Example 1.2.

Step 2 of the recipe. Given $\{Y=y\}$, $X\sim U(0,y)$, whose variance is $\frac{(y-0)^2}{12}=\frac{y^2}{12}$. Hence $$\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)=\frac{Y^2}{12},\qquad \mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]=\frac{Y}{2}.$$

Within term. We need $\mathbb{E}[Y^2]$ for $Y\sim U(0,\ell)$: $$\mathbb{E}[Y^2]=\int_0^{\ell}y^2\cdot\frac1\ell\,dy=\frac1\ell\left[\frac{y^3}{3}\right]_0^{\ell}=\frac1\ell\cdot\frac{\ell^3}{3}=\frac{\ell^2}{3},$$ so $\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[Y^2]}{12}=\frac{\ell^2/3}{12}=\frac{\ell^2}{36}\approx0.027778\,\ell^2$ (stick_Evar_coef).

Between term. $\operatorname{var}(Y)=\mathbb{E}[Y^2]-(\mathbb{E}[Y])^2=\frac{\ell^2}{3}-\frac{\ell^2}{4}=\frac{4\ell^2-3\ell^2}{12}=\frac{\ell^2}{12}$, the standard uniform variance, and $$\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)=\operatorname{var}\!\Big(\frac{Y}{2}\Big)=\frac14\operatorname{var}(Y)=\frac14\cdot\frac{\ell^2}{12}=\frac{\ell^2}{48}\approx0.020833\,\ell^2 .$$

Total. $$\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac{\ell^2}{36}+\frac{\ell^2}{48}=\ell^2\Big(\frac{4}{144}+\frac{3}{144}\Big)=\frac{7\ell^2}{144}\approx0.048611\,\ell^2 .$$ With $\ell=1$, four million simulations give $0.048538$, and integrating $x^2\ln(1/x)$ over $(0,1)$ gives $\mathbb{E}[X^2]=0.111111=\frac19$, so $\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac19-\frac1{16}=\frac{16-9}{144}=\frac7{144}$ ✓ (stick_varX_mc, stick_EX2_quad).

Reading it. The two contributions are comparable ($4:3$), so with this experiment neither "where the first break landed" nor "where the second break landed inside it" dominates.

Example 1.10 (the coin with random bias, variance; B&T Example 4.16 continued). $Y\sim U(0,1)$, $X\mid\{Y=y\}\sim\text{Bin}(n,y)$. Find $\operatorname{var}(X)$.

Within. A binomial with parameters $n$ and $y$ has variance $ny(1-y)$ (G2 §3), so $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)=nY(1-Y)$ and $$\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y)\big]=n\big(\mathbb{E}[Y]-\mathbb{E}[Y^2]\big)=n\Big(\frac12-\frac13\Big)=n\cdot\frac{3-2}{6}=\frac{n}{6},$$ using $\mathbb{E}[Y^2]=\operatorname{var}(Y)+(\mathbb{E}[Y])^2=\frac1{12}+\frac14=\frac{1+3}{12}=\frac13$.

Between. $\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y]\big)=\operatorname{var}(nY)=n^2\operatorname{var}(Y)=\frac{n^2}{12}$.

Total. $\displaystyle\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac{n}{6}+\frac{n^2}{12}$. For $n=10$: $\frac{10}{6}+\frac{100}{12}=\frac{5}{3}+\frac{25}{3}=\frac{30}{3}=10$ (coin_varX); simulation gives $9.990535$ ✓ (coin_varX_mc).

Interpretation. A fair coin would give variance $n\cdot\frac12\cdot\frac12=n/4=2.5$. The extra $\frac{n^2}{12}=8.33$ is entirely the between-group term: not knowing the bias hurts much more than the toss-to-toss noise, and its cost grows like $n^2$ rather than $n$, because a wrong bias biases every toss in the same direction.

Practice 1.6. Break the stick a third time: let $X_1=Y$, $X_2=X$ as in Example 1.2, and let $X_3$ be the left piece after breaking $X_2$ uniformly. Find $\mathbb{E}[X_3]$ and $\operatorname{var}(X_3)$.
Solution

Mean. $\mathbb{E}[X_3\mid X_2]=X_2/2$ by the same uniform argument as Example 1.2, so $\mathbb{E}[X_3]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[X_2]}{2}=\frac{\ell/4}{2}=\frac{\ell}{8}$ (pr4_EX3_coef). Each break halves the expected length — a pattern that continues: after $k$ breaks the mean is $\ell/2^k$.

Between term. $\operatorname{var}(X_3\mid X_2)$-free part first: $\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[X_3\mid X_2]\big)=\operatorname{var}(X_2/2)=\frac14\operatorname{var}(X_2)=\frac14\cdot\frac{7\ell^2}{144}=\frac{7\ell^2}{576}\approx0.012153\,\ell^2$ (pr4_varE_coef).

Within term. $\operatorname{var}(X_3\mid X_2)=\frac{X_2^2}{12}$, and $\mathbb{E}[X_2^2]=\operatorname{var}(X_2)+(\mathbb{E}[X_2])^2=\frac{7\ell^2}{144}+\frac{\ell^2}{16}=\frac{7\ell^2+9\ell^2}{144}=\frac{16\ell^2}{144}=\frac{\ell^2}{9}$, so $$\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{var}(X_3\mid X_2)\big]=\frac{\ell^2/9}{12}=\frac{\ell^2}{108}\approx0.009259\,\ell^2 .$$

Total. $\operatorname{var}(X_3)=\frac{7\ell^2}{576}+\frac{\ell^2}{108}=\ell^2\big(\frac{21}{1728}+\frac{16}{1728}\big)=\frac{37\ell^2}{1728}\approx0.021412\,\ell^2$; three million simulations with $\ell=1$ give $0.021353$ ✓ (pr4_varX3_coef, pr4_varX3_mc).

Note that conditioning on $X_2$ (the immediately preceding stage) was enough — you never needed the joint distribution of $(X_1,X_2,X_3)$. That "condition on the last thing that happened" habit is what makes Markov chains (G5) tractable.

1.5 Recitation 12, worked in full

Recitation 12 is a consolidation session: it revisits the correlation coefficient and the derived-distribution techniques of G3 §§2–4 rather than the two new laws above. It is worked here in full because it is the recitation attached to L12, and because three of its four problems are exactly the machinery §§1.1–1.4 lean on — affine rescaling of a variance, the density of a difference, and the Schwarz inequality that makes correlation coefficients bounded. Where the conditional-expectation viewpoint shortens a computation, that route is given as well.

Example 1.11 (rec12 P1 — the correlation coefficient is unchanged by an affine change of units with positive scale). Show that $\rho(aX+b,\,Y)=\rho(X,Y)$.
Solution

Definitions (B&T §4.2, G3 §4). $\operatorname{cov}(U,V)=\mathbb{E}\big[(U-\mathbb{E}[U])(V-\mathbb{E}[V])\big]$ and, when both variances are positive, $$\rho(U,V)=\frac{\operatorname{cov}(U,V)}{\sqrt{\operatorname{var}(U)\operatorname{var}(V)}} .$$ Here $a\ne0$ and $b$ are constants: $a$ is a change of scale (points out of $105$ instead of out of $100$), $b$ a shift of origin (a curve added to everyone's mark).

Preliminary — the [SOLUTION GAP] the official solution uses without proof. $\mathbb{E}[aX+b]=a\mathbb{E}[X]+b$ by linearity, so the deviation of $aX+b$ from its own mean is $$(aX+b)-\mathbb{E}[aX+b]=aX+b-a\mathbb{E}[X]-b=a\big(X-\mathbb{E}[X]\big),$$ the $b$'s cancelling exactly. Squaring and taking expectations, $$\operatorname{var}(aX+b)=\mathbb{E}\Big[a^2\big(X-\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2\Big]=a^2\operatorname{var}(X).$$

Main computation. Substituting $U=aX+b$, $V=Y$: $$\rho(aX+b,Y)=\frac{\operatorname{cov}(aX+b,Y)}{\sqrt{\operatorname{var}(aX+b)\operatorname{var}(Y)}} =\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[a(X-\mathbb{E}[X])(Y-\mathbb{E}[Y])\big]}{\sqrt{a^2\operatorname{var}(X)\operatorname{var}(Y)}} =\frac{a\,\operatorname{cov}(X,Y)}{|a|\sqrt{\operatorname{var}(X)\operatorname{var}(Y)}} =\operatorname{sign}(a)\,\rho(X,Y).$$ Each step: the numerator used the deviation identity above and pulled the constant $a$ out of the expectation; the denominator used $\operatorname{var}(aX+b)=a^2\operatorname{var}(X)$ and $\sqrt{a^2}=|a|$.

[SOURCE TYPO?] The official solution writes $\sqrt{a^2\operatorname{var}(X)\operatorname{var}(Y)}=a\sqrt{\operatorname{var}(X)\operatorname{var}(Y)}$, i.e. $\sqrt{a^2}=a$. That holds only for $a>0$. For $a>0$ the claim $\rho(aX+b,Y)=\rho(X,Y)$ is exactly right; for $a<0$ the correct statement is $\rho(aX+b,Y)=-\rho(X,Y)$, since reversing the direction of a scale reverses the sign of every association. (The first line of the official solution also writes $\sqrt{\operatorname{var}(aX+b)(\operatorname{var}(Y))}$ with a redundant pair of parentheses; harmless.)

Numerical illustration. Five students with homework scores $(70,85,90,60,95)$ and exam scores $(65,80,88,55,92)$ have $\operatorname{cov}=182$, $\operatorname{var}(X)=170$, $\operatorname{var}(Y)=195.6$ and $\rho=0.998073$. Rescaling the homework to a $105$-point scale and subtracting a $3$-point handicap, $a=1.05$, $b=-3$, leaves $\rho=0.998073$ unchanged; using $a=-1.05$ instead gives $-0.998073$ (corr_rho, corr_rho_affine, corr_rho_negative_a).

Why this matters. $\rho$ is dimensionless: it is unchanged by the units in which either variable is recorded. Covariance is not — measuring in points instead of percentage points multiplies it by $a$. That is why $\rho$, not $\operatorname{cov}$, is the number quoted when comparing associations measured in different units.

Example 1.12 (rec12 P2 — Romeo and Juliet: the PDF of a difference of exponentials). Romeo and Juliet are each late by independent amounts $X$ and $Y$, both exponential with parameter $\lambda$. Let $Z=X-Y$ be the amount by which Romeo is later than Juliet. (a) Find $f_Z$ via the CDF. (b) Find $f_Z$ by the total probability theorem.
Solution

Setup. $f_X(x)=\lambda e^{-\lambda x}$ for $x\ge0$ and $0$ for $x<0$; same for $Y$; independence gives $f_{X,Y}(x,y)=\lambda e^{-\lambda x}\lambda e^{-\lambda y}$ on the first quadrant. $Z$ can be any real number.

(a) CDF method, case $z\ge0$. The event is $$\{Z\le z\}=\{X-Y\le z\}=\{X\le Y+z\},$$ which in the $(x,y)$ plane is the part of the first quadrant lying above the line $y=x-z$ (Fig. 1.6a). Integrate $y$ from $0$ to $\infty$ and, for each $y$, $x$ from $0$ to $y+z$ (positive because $y\ge0$ and $z\ge0$): $$F_Z(z)=\int_0^{\infty}\lambda e^{-\lambda y}\left(\int_0^{y+z}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}dx\right)dy =\int_0^{\infty}\lambda e^{-\lambda y}\Big(1-e^{-\lambda(y+z)}\Big)dy,$$ where the inner integral is the exponential CDF, $\int_0^{t}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}dx=1-e^{-\lambda t}$ with $t=y+z$.

Filling the [SOLUTION GAP] — the source jumps straight to an antiderivative. Expand the integrand: $$\lambda e^{-\lambda y}-\lambda e^{-\lambda y}e^{-\lambda(y+z)}=\lambda e^{-\lambda y}-\lambda e^{-\lambda z}e^{-2\lambda y}.$$ Integrate the two pieces separately over $[0,\infty)$: $$\int_0^\infty\lambda e^{-\lambda y}dy=1,\qquad \int_0^\infty \lambda e^{-\lambda z}e^{-2\lambda y}dy=\lambda e^{-\lambda z}\left[\frac{e^{-2\lambda y}}{-2\lambda}\right]_0^{\infty} =\lambda e^{-\lambda z}\cdot\frac{1}{2\lambda}=\frac{e^{-\lambda z}}{2},$$ using $e^{-2\lambda y}\to0$ as $y\to\infty$ and $e^{0}=1$ at the lower limit. Hence $$F_Z(z)=1-\frac12e^{-\lambda z},\qquad z\ge0 .$$ (The source prints this as an antiderivative "$1+\frac{e^{-\lambda z}}{2}e^{-2\lambda y}$". That leading $1$ is not a constant of integration: it is the already-evaluated first piece $\int_0^\infty\lambda e^{-\lambda y}dy=1$, so the expression is a mixture of an evaluated integral and an unevaluated one and cannot be fed to $[\;\cdot\;]_0^\infty$ as printed. The genuine antiderivative of $\lambda e^{-\lambda y}-\lambda e^{-\lambda z}e^{-2\lambda y}$ is $-e^{-\lambda y}+\frac{e^{-\lambda z}}{2}e^{-2\lambda y}$, and $$\left[-e^{-\lambda y}+\frac{e^{-\lambda z}}{2}e^{-2\lambda y}\right]_0^{\infty}=0-\left(-1+\frac{e^{-\lambda z}}{2}\right)=1-\frac12e^{-\lambda z},$$ the same number as the two-piece computation above.)

[SOURCE TYPO?] The fourth line of the official solution prints "$\lambda^{\lambda y}\big(1-e^{-\lambda(y+z)}\big)dy$", omitting the integral sign and mis-setting $\lambda e^{-\lambda y}$; and its second line writes the integrand as $f_{X,Y}(x,y')$ with a spurious prime. Both are typesetting slips.

(a) case $z<0$. Now $y+z$ can be negative, so the same slicing would need care; slice the other way instead — [SOLUTION GAP]: the official solution swaps the order of integration silently. The region $\{x\le y+z,\ x\ge0,\ y\ge0\}$ can be described as: $x$ ranges over $[0,\infty)$ and, for each such $x$, $y$ ranges over $[x-z,\infty)$ (from $x\le y+z\iff y\ge x-z$; and $x-z>x\ge0$ automatically, so the constraint $y\ge0$ is implied). Then $$F_Z(z)=\int_0^{\infty}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}\left(\int_{x-z}^{\infty}\lambda e^{-\lambda y}dy\right)dx =\int_0^{\infty}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}e^{-\lambda(x-z)}dx =e^{\lambda z}\int_0^{\infty}\lambda e^{-2\lambda x}dx=e^{\lambda z}\cdot\frac12=\frac12e^{\lambda z},$$ using the exponential tail $\int_t^\infty \lambda e^{-\lambda y}dy=e^{-\lambda t}$ with $t=x-z$, and $\int_0^\infty\lambda e^{-2\lambda x}dx=\frac{\lambda}{2\lambda}=\frac12$.

Differentiate. $$F_Z(z)=\begin{cases}1-\frac12e^{-\lambda z},&z\ge0\\[2pt]\frac12e^{\lambda z},&z<0\end{cases} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad f_Z(z)=\begin{cases}\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{-\lambda z},&z\ge0\\[2pt]\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{\lambda z},&z<0\end{cases} \;=\;\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{-\lambda|z|}.$$ This is the Laplace (double-exponential) density. Continuity check at $z=0$: both branches of $F_Z$ give $\frac12$, and both branches of $f_Z$ give $\lambda/2$ ✓. [SOURCE TYPO?] the boxed conclusion in the official solution prints $\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{\lambda|z|}$, missing the minus sign; that function is not integrable, and part (b) of the same solution has the sign right.

(b) Total probability / convolution route. Condition on $X$ and integrate: $$f_Z(z)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_X(x)\,f_{Z\mid X}(z\mid x)\,dx .$$ [SOLUTION GAP] Given $\{X=x\}$, $Z=x-Y$ is an affine function of $Y$ with slope $-1$, so by the linear-transformation rule of G3 §4, $f_{Z\mid X}(z\mid x)=\frac{1}{|-1|}f_{Y\mid X}(x-z\mid x)=f_{Y\mid X}(x-z\mid x)$; independence then removes the conditioning, $f_{Y\mid X}(\cdot\mid x)=f_Y(\cdot)$. Hence $$f_Z(z)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_X(x)f_Y(x-z)\,dx,$$ which is the convolution formula of G3 §4 applied to $X$ and $-Y$.

Case $z<0$. [SOLUTION GAP] $f_X(x)=0$ unless $x\ge0$, and for $z<0$ the second factor's argument $x-z=x+|z|$ is automatically $\ge0$; so the effective range is $x\in[0,\infty)$: $$f_Z(z)=\int_0^{\infty}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}\,\lambda e^{-\lambda(x-z)}dx=\lambda e^{\lambda z}\int_0^{\infty}\lambda e^{-2\lambda x}dx=\lambda e^{\lambda z}\cdot\frac12=\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{\lambda z}.$$

Case $z\ge0$. [SOLUTION GAP] Now $f_Y(x-z)=0$ unless $x\ge z$, so the lower limit becomes $z$: $$f_Z(z)=\int_z^{\infty}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}\lambda e^{-\lambda(x-z)}dx=\lambda e^{\lambda z}\int_z^{\infty}\lambda e^{-2\lambda x}dx =\lambda e^{\lambda z}\cdot\frac{e^{-2\lambda z}}{2}=\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{-\lambda z},$$ using $\int_z^\infty\lambda e^{-2\lambda x}dx=\big[\frac{e^{-2\lambda x}}{-2}\big]_z^\infty=\frac{e^{-2\lambda z}}{2}$. Combining, $f_Z(z)=\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{-\lambda|z|}$ for all $z$ — the same answer as (a).

Checks. With $\lambda=1$: numerical integration gives total mass $1.000000$, $\mathbb{E}[Z]=0.000000$ and $\operatorname{var}(Z)=2.000000$; three million simulated pairs give $\mathbb{E}[Z]=-0.001052$, $\operatorname{var}(Z)=2.000319$ (rj_norm, rj_var, rj_var_mc). The variance also follows in one line without any density: $X$ and $Y$ are independent, so $\operatorname{var}(Z)=\operatorname{var}(X)+\operatorname{var}(-Y)=\frac1{\lambda^2}+\frac1{\lambda^2}=\frac{2}{\lambda^2}$ ✓ (rj_var_indep). Also $\mathbf{P}(Z>0)=\frac12$ by symmetry, and $\mathbf{P}(|Z|\le 1/\lambda)=1-e^{-1}=0.632121$ (rj_P_pos, rj_P_abs_le_1).

Interpretation. The memorylessness of the exponential shows up as the pure exponential decay on each side of $0$: given that Romeo is already later than Juliet, the extra amount is again exponential with the same parameter.

Fig. 1.6 — rec12 P2: (a) the event $\{Z\le z\}$ as a region of the plane (left panel); (b) the resulting Laplace density $f_Z(z)=\frac{\lambda}{2}e^{-\lambda|z|}$ ($\lambda=1$), with $\operatorname{var}(Z)=2/\lambda^2$ (right panel).
Example 1.13 (rec12 P3 — polar coordinates of a standard normal pair; B&T Problem 4.16, p. 248). Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent standard normal random variables and write $X=R\cos\Theta$, $Y=R\sin\Theta$ with $R\ge0$ and $\Theta\in[0,2\pi]$. Find (a) $f_R$, (b) $f_\Theta$, (c) $f_{R,\Theta}$, and show $R$ and $\Theta$ are independent.
Solution

The joint density in polar form — [SOLUTION GAP] filled. By independence, $$f_{X,Y}(x,y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^2/2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-y^2/2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-(x^2+y^2)/2}.$$ Substituting $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$ and using $\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=1$ gives $x^2+y^2=r^2$, so $$f_{X,Y}(r\cos\theta,\;r\sin\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-r^2/2},$$ which depends on $r$ only — the density is circularly symmetric (Fig. 1.7a). The area element in polar coordinates is $dx\,dy=r\,dr\,d\theta$; the extra factor $r$ is the Jacobian and is the whole content of the computation.

(a) The radius. The event $\{R\le r\}$ is the disc of radius $r$: $$F_R(r)=\int_0^{r}\!\!\int_0^{2\pi}\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-(r')^2/2}\,d\theta\;r'\,dr' =\left(\int_0^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\right)\int_0^{r}r'e^{-(r')^2/2}\,dr' =1\cdot\int_0^{r}r'e^{-(r')^2/2}\,dr' .$$ Substitute $u=(r')^2/2$, so $du=r'\,dr'$ and the limits $r'=0,r$ become $u=0,r^2/2$: $$F_R(r)=\int_0^{r^2/2}e^{-u}du=\big[-e^{-u}\big]_0^{r^2/2}=-e^{-r^2/2}+1=1-e^{-r^2/2},\qquad r\ge0$$ (and $F_R(r)=0$ for $r<0$) — the evaluation the source skips. Differentiating by the chain rule, $$f_R(r)=\frac{d}{dr}\Big(1-e^{-r^2/2}\Big)=-e^{-r^2/2}\cdot\Big(-\frac{2r}{2}\Big)=r\,e^{-r^2/2},\qquad r\ge0 .$$ This is the Rayleigh density. [SOURCE TYPO?] the official solution writes this derivative as $(-1/2)(2r)(-e^{-r^2/2})$, which is numerically right but grouped as though one were differentiating $-e^{-r^2/2}$.

(b) The angle. Slicing the plane by angle instead of radius, $$F_\Theta(\theta)=\int_0^{\theta}\!\!\int_0^{\infty}\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-r^2/2}\,r\,dr\,d\theta' =\left(\int_0^{\theta}\frac{d\theta'}{2\pi}\right)\int_0^{\infty}re^{-r^2/2}dr=\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\int_0^{\infty}e^{-u}du=\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\cdot 1=\frac{\theta}{2\pi}$$ for $0\le\theta\le2\pi$ (same substitution $u=r^2/2$), with $F_\Theta=0$ below $0$ and $1$ above $2\pi$. Differentiating, $f_\Theta(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}$ on $[0,2\pi]$: the angle is uniform.

(c) The joint density. $$F_{R,\Theta}(r,\theta)=\int_0^{\theta}\!\!\int_0^{r}\frac{1}{2\pi}r'e^{-(r')^2/2}dr'\,d\theta' =\int_0^{\theta}\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big(1-e^{-r^2/2}\Big)d\theta'=\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\Big(1-e^{-r^2/2}\Big)$$ for $r\ge0$ and $0\le\theta\le2\pi$. [SOURCE TYPO?] the source labels this branch "$r\ge0,\ \theta>2\pi$"; the case list immediately below it (and the derivation) show the branch is the one for $0\le\theta\le2\pi$. Differentiating once in each variable, $$f_{R,\Theta}(r,\theta)=\frac{\partial^2}{\partial r\,\partial\theta}F_{R,\Theta}(r,\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\,r\,e^{-r^2/2}.$$

Conclusion — the thing the problem actually asked, which the official solution never states ([SOLUTION GAP]). Comparing with (a) and (b), $$f_{R,\Theta}(r,\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}re^{-r^2/2}=\Big(re^{-r^2/2}\Big)\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big)=f_R(r)\,f_\Theta(\theta)\quad\text{for all }r\ge0,\ 0\le\theta\le2\pi,$$ so by the definition of independence for continuous random variables (G3 §2), $R$ and $\Theta$ are independent. A grid check of the two sides agrees to machine precision (ray_factor_maxerr $=0.000000$).

Numbers. $\int_0^\infty re^{-r^2/2}dr=1.000000$ ✓; $\mathbb{E}[R]=\sqrt{\pi/2}\approx1.253314$; $\mathbb{E}[R^2]=2$ exactly, so $\operatorname{var}(R)=2-\frac{\pi}{2}\approx0.429204$; the median radius is $\sqrt{2\ln2}\approx1.177410$ and $\mathbf{P}(R\le1)=1-e^{-1/2}\approx0.393469$. Two million simulated normal pairs give $\mathbb{E}[R]=1.253245$ and $\mathbb{E}[R^2]=1.999536$ ✓ (ray_ER, ray_varR, ray_ER_mc).

Why anyone cares (the recitation's closing note). $R^2$ has CDF $\mathbf{P}(R^2\le t)=\mathbf{P}(R\le\sqrt t)=1-e^{-t/2}$, i.e. $R^2$ is exponential with parameter $\lambda=1/2$ — mean $2$, matching $\mathbb{E}[R^2]=2$ above. So to generate a standard normal pair: draw $T$ exponential$(1/2)$ and $\Theta$ uniform on $[0,2\pi]$ independently, and set $X=\sqrt T\cos\Theta$, $Y=\sqrt T\sin\Theta$. This is the Box–Muller method, and it exists because the normal CDF has no closed-form inverse, so the usual inverse-CDF trick of G3 §4 is unavailable. [SOURCE TYPO?] the note says "the PDF of $R^2$ is exponentially distributed"; a PDF is not distributed — $R^2$ is.

Fig. 1.7 — rec12 P3: (a) circular symmetry of the standard normal pair (left panel); (b) the two independent marginals $f_R(r)=re^{-r^2/2}$ and $f_\Theta(\theta)=1/2\pi$ (right panel). B&T Problem 4.16.
Example 1.14 (rec12 P4 — the Schwarz inequality; B&T Problem 4.20, p. 250). Show that $\big(\mathbb{E}[XY]\big)^2\le\mathbb{E}[X^2]\,\mathbb{E}[Y^2]$ for any random variables $X,Y$.
Solution

Idea. Try to approximate $X$ by a multiple $\alpha Y$ of $Y$ and see how good the best approximation can be. Define, for a real parameter $\alpha$, $$J(\alpha)=\mathbb{E}\big[(X-\alpha Y)^2\big].$$ Being the expectation of a square, $J(\alpha)\ge0$ for every $\alpha$ — that single fact is the whole inequality.

Expand ([SOLUTION GAP]: the source uses this expansion silently). $(X-\alpha Y)^2=X^2-2\alpha XY+\alpha^2Y^2$, so by linearity of expectation $$J(\alpha)=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-2\alpha\,\mathbb{E}[XY]+\alpha^2\,\mathbb{E}[Y^2],$$ a quadratic in $\alpha$ with leading coefficient $\mathbb{E}[Y^2]$.

Minimize ([SOLUTION GAP]: the differentiation is skipped in the source). Assume $\mathbb{E}[Y^2]>0$. Then $$\frac{dJ}{d\alpha}=-2\,\mathbb{E}[XY]+2\alpha\,\mathbb{E}[Y^2]=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \alpha^{*}=\frac{\mathbb{E}[XY]}{\mathbb{E}[Y^2]},$$ and $\frac{d^2J}{d\alpha^2}=2\,\mathbb{E}[Y^2]>0$, so this stationary point is the unique minimum (the parabola of the recitation's figure).
