$0.01$$0.980199$$0.98$$0.000199$$0.000197$$0.019735$
$0.001$$0.998002$$0.998$$0.000002$$0.000002$$0.001997$

Reading. Cutting $\delta$ by a factor of $10$ cuts the error in $P(0,\delta)$ by about $100$ — the error is $O(\delta^2)$, which is what "$o(\delta)$" is doing here. The last column is the honest test of "negligible compared to $\delta$": $P(k\ge2,\delta)/\delta$ itself goes to zero, roughly like $\lambda^2\delta/2$. At $\delta=0.5$ the linear formula is worthless ($1-\lambda\delta=0$, a probability that is off by $0.37$); the properties are statements about the limit, not licences to use $\lambda\delta$ at any width you like.

Gotcha. $\lambda\delta$ is a probability only when $\lambda\delta\le1$. $\lambda$ has units of $1/\text{time}$, so "$\lambda=5$" is meaningless until you say $5$ per what. Always convert the rate and the window to the same time unit before multiplying: $\lambda=5$ per hour over $\tau=30$ minutes gives $\lambda\tau=5\times\frac12=2.5$, not $5\times30$. Because $\lambda\tau$ is the expected number of arrivals, a value bigger than $1$ is perfectly fine — it is $\lambda\delta$ in property 3, with $\delta\to0$, that must be small.
Practice 3.1. Requests hit a server as a Poisson process with $\lambda=4$ per second. Using property 3, approximate the probability of exactly one request in a window of $\delta=0.002$ seconds, and compare with the exact value.
Solution

Property 3 gives $P(1,\delta)\approx\lambda\delta=4\times0.002=0.008$. The exact value (from §3.2) is $P(1,\delta)=\lambda\delta e^{-\lambda\delta}=0.008\,e^{-0.008}=0.00793626$. The relative error is $|0.008-0.00793626|/0.00793626=0.008032$, i.e. about $0.8\%$ — and note that it is itself of size $\lambda\delta$, as the Taylor expansion $e^{-\lambda\delta}=1-\lambda\delta+\cdots$ predicts (pq_31_*).

Practice 3.2. For each stream below, say which defining property fails. (a) Buses that are scheduled every 10 minutes and are almost always on time. (b) Calls to a help desk over a full 24-hour day. (c) Goals in a football match, where a goal is often immediately followed by a second one in the celebration-and-restart chaos. (d) Twins born at a maternity ward, where a "birth event" delivers two babies at the same instant.
Solution

(a) Independence fails hardest — knowing a bus just came tells you the next is 10 minutes away. (Time homogeneity also fails at the level of the timetable.) (b) Time homogeneity fails: $P(k,\tau)$ for a one-hour window at 3 p.m. is not the law for a one-hour window at 3 a.m. The usual repair is a nonhomogeneous Poisson process with a time-varying $\lambda(t)$ (beyond 6.041). (c) Independence fails: arrivals cluster. (d) Small-interval probabilities fail: $P(2,\delta)$ does not vanish faster than $\delta$, because two arrivals genuinely coincide. The standard repair is to model events as Poisson and attach a random batch size — that is precisely the random sum of §2.

3.2 The number of arrivals: binomial $\to$ Poisson, derived

Fix a window length $t>0$ and let $N_t$ be the number of arrivals in it. We compute the PMF of $N_t$ by discretizing, using the binomial answer we already have, and taking a limit. This is L14 slide 4 and B&T §6.1, its Figure 6.5.

Step 1 — the discrete approximation

Chop $[0,t]$ into $n$ slots each of length $\delta=t/n$. Here $n$ is a large positive integer, our only free knob; $\delta$ is the slot width. By property 3, each slot contains one arrival with probability approximately

$$p=\lambda\delta=\lambda\frac tn,$$

no arrival with probability approximately $1-p$, and two or more with probability negligible compared with $\delta$. By property 2, distinct slots are independent, and by property 1 they all carry the same $p$. So the $n$ slots are $n$ independent Bernoulli trials, and the count

$$\tilde N_n=\#\{\text{slots containing an arrival}\}\ \sim\ \text{binomial}(n,p),\qquad \mathbf{P}(\tilde N_n=k)=\binom nk p^k(1-p)^{n-k}.$$

Two things to notice before we take the limit. First, $\tilde N_n$ undercounts $N_t$ whenever a slot holds two or more arrivals — and property 3 says that source of error washes out as $n\to\infty$. Second, the product

$$np=n\cdot\lambda\frac tn=\lambda t$$

does not depend on $n$ at all. That is the whole trick: refine the discretization and the success probability shrinks exactly as fast as the number of trials grows, so the expected number of arrivals stays pinned at $\lambda t$. Write $\mu=\lambda t$ for that fixed product; it is the only quantity that will survive.

Step 2 — the limit, every factor accounted for

Fix $k$ and substitute $p=\mu/n$:

$$\mathbf{P}(\tilde N_n=k)=\frac{n!}{k!\,(n-k)!}\left(\frac\mu n\right)^k\left(1-\frac\mu n\right)^{n-k}.$$

Expand $n!/(n-k)!=n(n-1)\cdots(n-k+1)$ — a product of exactly $k$ factors — and split the last power as $(1-\mu/n)^{n-k}=(1-\mu/n)^{n}\,(1-\mu/n)^{-k}$:

$$\mathbf{P}(\tilde N_n=k) =\underbrace{\frac{n(n-1)\cdots(n-k+1)}{n^k}}_{A_n} \cdot\underbrace{\frac{\mu^k}{k!}}_{\text{no }n} \cdot\underbrace{\left(1-\frac\mu n\right)^{n}}_{B_n} \cdot\underbrace{\left(1-\frac\mu n\right)^{-k}}_{C_n},$$

where the $n^k$ in $A_n$ came out of $(\mu/n)^k=\mu^k/n^k$. Now take $n\to\infty$ with $k$ and $\mu$ held fixed, one factor at a time.

Factor $A_n$. Divide each of the $k$ numerator factors by one copy of $n$: $$A_n=\frac nn\cdot\frac{n-1}n\cdots\frac{n-k+1}n=\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\Big(1-\frac jn\Big).$$ This is a product of a fixed number $k$ of terms, each of which tends to $1$, so $A_n\to1$. (It matters that $k$ is fixed; if $k$ grew with $n$ the argument would collapse.)

Factor $B_n$. Take logarithms and expand: $$\ln B_n=n\ln\Big(1-\frac\mu n\Big) =n\left(-\frac\mu n-\frac{\mu^2}{2n^2}-\frac{\mu^3}{3n^3}-\cdots\right) =-\mu-\frac{\mu^2}{2n}-\frac{\mu^3}{3n^2}-\cdots\ \longrightarrow\ -\mu,$$ using the series $\ln(1-x)=-x-x^2/2-x^3/3-\cdots$, valid for $|x|<1$, which applies once $n>\mu$. Every term after the first carries a positive power of $1/n$ and dies. Exponentiating (the exponential is continuous, so limits pass through it), $B_n\to e^{-\mu}$. This is the familiar $\lim_{n\to\infty}(1-\mu/n)^n=e^{-\mu}$, as B&T note in the footnote to §6.1.

Factor $C_n$. $(1-\mu/n)^{-k}$ is a fixed power $-k$ of something tending to $1$, so $C_n\to1$.

Multiplying the four limits (the product of convergent sequences converges to the product of the limits):

$$\boxed{\ \mathbf{P}(N_t=k)=P(k,t)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}(\tilde N_n=k) =\frac{\mu^k e^{-\mu}}{k!}=\frac{(\lambda t)^k e^{-\lambda t}}{k!},\qquad k=0,1,2,\dots\ }$$

which is L14 slide 4's formula, written there with $\tau$ in place of $t$. The intermediate values at $n=100$, $k=2$, $\mu=2.5$ make the four factors concrete (lim_factor_*): $A_{100}=\frac{100\cdot99}{100^2}=0.99$, $\mu^k/k!=2.5^2/2=3.125$, $B_{100}=(1-0.025)^{100}=0.0795173$ against the limit $e^{-2.5}=0.0820850$, and $C_{100}=(1-0.025)^{-2}=1.0519395$. Their product is $0.99\times3.125\times0.0795173\times1.0519395=0.2587841$, which is exactly the binomial value $\binom{100}{2}(0.025)^2(0.975)^{98}=0.2587841$; the Poisson limit is $0.2565156$.

Property (number of arrivals; L14 slide 4, B&T §6.2, p. 311). In a Poisson process of rate $\lambda$, the number $N_\tau$ of arrivals in any window of total length $\tau$ is a Poisson random variable with parameter $\lambda\tau$: $$P(k,\tau)=\frac{(\lambda\tau)^ke^{-\lambda\tau}}{k!},\qquad \mathbb{E}[N_\tau]=\lambda\tau,\qquad \operatorname{var}(N_\tau)=\lambda\tau .$$

Step 3 — the mean and the variance (L14 slide 4's second [DERIVATION GAP])

The slide asserts $\mathbb{E}[N_t]=\lambda t$ and $\operatorname{var}(N_t)=\lambda t$ without argument. Both drop out of the same limit. From G2 the binomial $\tilde N_n$ has

$$\mathbb{E}[\tilde N_n]=np=\lambda t\ \ \text{(exactly, for every }n),\qquad \operatorname{var}(\tilde N_n)=np(1-p)=\lambda t\Big(1-\frac{\lambda t}{n}\Big).$$

The mean needs no limit at all — it is $\lambda t$ for every discretization, which is the sanity check that $\lambda$ deserves the name "rate". The variance carries a correction factor $1-\lambda t/n$ that tends to $1$, so $\operatorname{var}(N_t)=\lambda t$. Equivalently, verify it directly from the PMF; computes/g4_s3.py sums the Poisson PMF numerically and gets $\sum_k P(k,\tau)=1.000000$, mean $=\lambda\tau$ and variance $=\lambda\tau$ to eight decimals for $\lambda\tau=0.5,\,2.5,\,5$ (pois_sum_*, pois_mean_*, pois_var_*).

Interpretation. Mean equals variance is the fingerprint of the Poisson. The standard deviation is $\sqrt{\lambda\tau}$, so the relative spread $\sqrt{\lambda\tau}/(\lambda\tau)=1/\sqrt{\lambda\tau}$ shrinks as the window grows: over a long window the count is close to $\lambda\tau$ in percentage terms, over a short one it is wildly variable. Doubling $\tau$ doubles the mean but only multiplies the standard deviation by $\sqrt2$ — the same $\sqrt{n}$ effect that runs through the whole course.
Fig. 3.2 — The limit in action, $\lambda t=2.5$ throughout. Left: the binomial PMF closes on the Poisson as the slots get finer. Right: the largest gap between the two PMFs falls like $1/n$ — from $0.098737$ at $n=5$ to $0.000294$ at $n=1000$ (lim_maxdev_*) (L14 slide 4; B&T §6.1, its Figure 6.5).
Fig. 3.3 — The Poisson family $P(k,\tau)=(\lambda\tau)^ke^{-\lambda\tau}/k!$. Small $\lambda\tau$: a spike at $0$ and a fast decay. Large $\lambda\tau$: a bell centred at $\lambda\tau$ with spread $\sqrt{\lambda\tau}$ (L14 slide 4; B&T §6.2).

The window does not have to be an interval

Property (any time set of total length $\tau$; B&T §6.2, Example 6.9). If $S$ is any union of finitely many disjoint intervals with total length $\tau$, the number of arrivals in $S$ is Poisson with parameter $\lambda\tau$.

Why. Suppose $S=[a_1,b_1]\cup[a_2,b_2]$ with the two intervals disjoint, of lengths $\tau_1$ and $\tau_2$, $\tau_1+\tau_2=\tau$. By property 2 the two counts $M$ and $N$ are independent, and by property 1 and the theorem above they are Poisson with parameters $\lambda\tau_1$ and $\lambda\tau_2$. Rather than convolve (G3 §4 would let you), use B&T's shifting argument: slide the second interval so that it sits immediately to the right of the first. Time homogeneity says the shifted count $\tilde N$ has the same distribution as $N$, and independence says $\tilde N$ is still independent of $M$; so $M+N$ has the same distribution as $M+\tilde N$. But $M+\tilde N$ counts arrivals in one interval of length $\tau_1+\tau_2=\tau$, hence is Poisson $(\lambda\tau)$. B&T Example 6.9 does exactly this with customers arriving at rate $10$ per minute: $M$ over $9{:}00$–$9{:}10$ is Poisson$(100)$, $N$ over $9{:}30$–$9{:}35$ is Poisson$(50)$, and $M+N$ is Poisson$(150)$ (bank_mu_*) even though the time set is not an interval.

Example 3.2 (L14 slide 5 — the email example, left blank on the slide [UNSOLVED EXAMPLE]). You get email according to a Poisson process at a rate of $\lambda=5$ messages per hour. You check your email every thirty minutes. Find the probability of no new messages and the probability of exactly one new message.

Solution. Step 1 — get the units right. The rate is per hour, so express the window in hours: $\tau=30\text{ min}=\tfrac12$ hour. Then $$\lambda\tau=5\times\tfrac12=2.5 ,$$ the expected number of messages per check.

Step 2 — apply the PMF. With $\mu=\lambda\tau=2.5$, $$\mathbf{P}(\text{no new messages})=P(0,\tfrac12)=\frac{2.5^0e^{-2.5}}{0!}=e^{-2.5}=0.082085,$$ $$\mathbf{P}(\text{one new message})=P(1,\tfrac12)=\frac{2.5^1e^{-2.5}}{1!}=2.5\,e^{-2.5}=0.205212,$$ using $0!=1!=1$ and $2.5^0=1$ (mail_P0, mail_P1).

Step 3 — read the answer. An empty inbox happens about $8\%$ of the time; exactly one message about $21\%$; and $P(2,\tfrac12)=\frac{2.5^2}{2}e^{-2.5}=0.256516$ is the most likely single outcome. Two or more messages has probability $1-0.082085-0.205212=0.712703$ (mail_Pge2). Per check the count has mean $2.5$, variance $2.5$ and standard deviation $\sqrt{2.5}=1.581139$ — the spread is comparable to the mean, so "about two or three, give or take two" is the honest summary.

Cross-check against the book. B&T Example 6.8 is the same problem with $\lambda=0.2$ per hour and $\tau=1$ hour, giving $\lambda\tau=0.2$ and $P(0,1)=e^{-0.2}=0.818731$, $P(1,1)=0.2e^{-0.2}=0.163746$ (mail_bt_*). Same formula, different numbers — the lecture chose a busier inbox.

Example 3.3 (B&T Example 6.6 — when is the Poisson approximation any good?). Kasparov plays $n=100$ amateurs simultaneously and loses or draws each game independently with probability $p=0.01$. Compare the exact binomial probabilities for the number $X$ of non-wins with the Poisson approximation at $\mu=np=1$; then repeat with $n=5$, $p=0.1$, $\mu=0.5$.

Solution. The binomial values are $p_X(k)=\binom{100}{k}0.01^k0.99^{100-k}$ and the approximations $e^{-1}/k!$ (kas_*):

$k$$0$$2$$5$$10$
binomial $n=100$$0.366032$$0.184865$$0.0028978$$7.006\times10^{-8}$
Poisson $\mu=1$$0.367879$$0.183940$$0.0030657$$1.014\times10^{-7}$

The largest discrepancy anywhere in $k=0,\dots,10$ is $0.001850$ (kas100_maxdev) — three-decimal agreement. With $n=5$, $p=0.1$ the two PMFs are $(0.590490,0.328050,0.072900,0.008100,0.000450,0.000010)$ against $(0.606531,0.303265,0.075816,0.012636,0.001580,0.000158)$, a maximum gap of $0.024785$ (kas5_maxdev) — visibly worse, and the Poisson even assigns positive probability to $k>5$, which the binomial cannot. Rule of thumb (B&T §6.1): the approximation is good to several decimals once $n\ge100$ and $p\le0.01$ with $\mu=np$.

Recipe (counting questions in a Poisson process).
  1. Identify $\lambda$ and its time unit. Rewrite every duration in that unit.
  2. Add up the total length $\tau$ of the time set you care about — it need not be one interval, only the total matters.
  3. Form $\mu=\lambda\tau$. This single number is the whole distribution: mean, variance, and parameter.
  4. Answer with $P(k,\tau)=\mu^ke^{-\mu}/k!$, summing over $k$ for "at least/at most" questions and using the complement when that is shorter.
  5. If several disjoint windows appear, treat their counts as independent and multiply.

Widget: watch the limit happen

Practice 3.3. Buses arrive as a Poisson process at $\lambda=6$ per hour. You wait $20$ minutes. (a) What is the probability that no bus comes? (b) What is the probability that three or more come?
Solution

(a) Convert: $\tau=20$ min $=\tfrac13$ hour, so $\mu=\lambda\tau=6\times\tfrac13=2$. Then $P(0,\tau)=e^{-2}=0.135335$ (pq_32_P0).

(b) Use the complement, because "three or more" is an infinite sum but "two or fewer" is three terms: $$\mathbf{P}(N\ge3)=1-P(0)-P(1)-P(2)=1-e^{-2}\Big(1+2+\tfrac{2^2}{2}\Big)=1-5e^{-2}=0.323324$$ (pq_32_Pge3).

Practice 3.4. Same buses ($\lambda=6$/hour). Given that exactly two buses came in a particular $20$-minute window, what is the probability that exactly one came in each $10$-minute half?
Solution

Let $A$ be the first half and $B$ the second, each of length $\tfrac16$ hour so $\lambda\tau_A=\lambda\tau_B=1$. The counts $N_A,N_B$ are independent Poisson$(1)$ by properties 1–2. $$\mathbf{P}(N_A=1,N_B=1)=\big(1\cdot e^{-1}\big)^2=e^{-2}=0.135335,\qquad \mathbf{P}(N_A+N_B=2)=\frac{2^2e^{-2}}{2}=2e^{-2}=0.270671 .$$ Dividing, the conditional probability is $e^{-2}/(2e^{-2})=\tfrac12=0.5$ exactly (pq_32_cond). Why $\tfrac12$? Conditioned on the total count, each arrival lands in the first half or the second independently with probability $\tfrac12$ — the positions become i.i.d. uniform on the window. So the split is binomial$(2,\tfrac12)$ and the answer is $\binom21(\tfrac12)^2=\tfrac12$. This "condition on the total, positions are uniform" fact is worth remembering; it is the seed of the splitting results in §4.

3.3 The first interarrival time: the exponential

Counts answer "how many by time $t$". The complementary question is "how long until the next one". Let $T$ (equivalently $Y_1$, the time of the first arrival) be the time from an arbitrary starting instant, say $0$, to the first arrival after it. Because $T$ is a continuous random variable, we go through the CDF — the G3 §4 recipe — since a CDF is the probability of an event, and events we can compute with $P(k,\tau)$.

The key event identity. For $t>0$, $$\{T>t\}=\{\text{no arrival in }[0,t]\}=\{N_t=0\},$$ because the first arrival happening after $t$ says exactly that the interval $[0,t]$ is empty, and conversely. This is an equality of events, not an approximation. Hence

$$\mathbf{P}(T>t)=P(0,t)=\frac{(\lambda t)^0e^{-\lambda t}}{0!}=e^{-\lambda t},$$

using the count PMF at $k=0$. Therefore the CDF is

$$F_T(t)=\mathbf{P}(T\le t)=1-\mathbf{P}(T>t)=1-e^{-\lambda t},\qquad t\ge0,$$

and $F_T(t)=0$ for $t<0$ since $T$ is nonnegative. Differentiating (the PDF is the derivative of the CDF, G3 §1):

$$f_T(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\big(1-e^{-\lambda t}\big)=-\big(-\lambda e^{-\lambda t}\big)=\lambda e^{-\lambda t}, \qquad t\ge0,$$

by the chain rule on $e^{-\lambda t}$. So $T$ is exponential with parameter $\lambda$ — L14 slide 6, the $k=1$ case of the Erlang. From G3 §1 we already know $\mathbb{E}[T]=1/\lambda$ and $\operatorname{var}(T)=1/\lambda^2$.

Property (interarrival times; L14 slide 6, B&T §6.2, pp. 311, 315–316). In a Poisson process of rate $\lambda$ the interarrival times $T_1,T_2,T_3,\dots$ (with $T_1=Y_1$ and $T_k=Y_k-Y_{k-1}$ for $k\ge2$) are independent and each is exponential with parameter $\lambda$: $$f_{T_k}(t)=\lambda e^{-\lambda t}\ (t\ge0),\qquad \mathbb{E}[T_k]=\frac1\lambda,\qquad \operatorname{var}(T_k)=\frac1{\lambda^2}.$$ Conversely, a process built by drawing i.i.d. exponential gaps and recording arrivals at $T_1$, $T_1+T_2$, $T_1+T_2+T_3,\dots$ is a Poisson process (B&T's "alternative description", p. 316).

Why the gaps are independent and identically distributed. The derivation above used only that the process starts fresh at time $0$. By property 2 the process after any time $t$ is independent of everything before $t$ and, by property 1, obeys the same law — so it is again a Poisson process of rate $\lambda$ started at $t$. Apply the identity $\{T>s\}=\{N=0 \text{ on a window of length } s\}$ to that restarted process and $T_2$ is exponential$(\lambda)$ too, and is determined entirely by the post-$Y_1$ future, hence independent of $T_1$. Repeating gives the whole i.i.d. sequence. (B&T flag in a footnote that restarting at the random time $Y_1$ rather than a deterministic $t$ needs an extra conditioning argument; it is true, and intuitive, and 6.041 uses it freely.)

Memorylessness, and why it is the same statement

Property (memorylessness; L14 slide 6, B&T §6.2, p. 314). For any $t,s\ge0$, $$\mathbf{P}(T>t+s\mid T>t)=\mathbf{P}(T>s)=e^{-\lambda s}.$$ The time still to wait, given that you have already waited $t$, has the same exponential distribution as a fresh wait.

Derivation. The event $\{T>t+s\}$ is contained in $\{T>t\}$ (waiting past $t+s$ entails waiting past $t$), so their intersection is $\{T>t+s\}$ itself. By the definition of conditional probability (G1 §2),

$$\mathbf{P}(T>t+s\mid T>t)=\frac{\mathbf{P}(T>t+s)}{\mathbf{P}(T>t)} =\frac{e^{-\lambda(t+s)}}{e^{-\lambda t}}=e^{-\lambda(t+s)+\lambda t}=e^{-\lambda s},$$

using the law of exponents. With $\lambda=5$, $t=0.4$, $s=0.2$ the two sides are both $0.367879$, agreeing to $0$ in double precision (mem_cond, mem_fresh, mem_devi). Alternatively — and more revealingly — condition on the process rather than the random variable: $\mathbf{P}(T-t>s\mid \text{no arrival in }[0,t])$ concerns only the window $[t,t+s]$, which property 2 declares independent of $[0,t]$; so the answer is $P(0,s)=e^{-\lambda s}$ with no algebra at all.

Interpretation. Memorylessness is the continuous-time twin of the Bernoulli fact from G2 §3 that a geometric random variable "forgets" past failures. Both say the process carries no state: at every instant, the future looks statistically identical to the future at time zero. Practically it means you may restart your clock whenever you like — a licence used constantly in problems ("given that 10 minutes have passed with no bus…" is not extra information about the wait remaining).
Gotcha. Memorylessness says the remaining wait has the same distribution, not that the total wait does. Given $T>t$, the total wait $T$ has mean $t+1/\lambda$, not $1/\lambda$. Also: memorylessness is a statement about a single exponential gap, not about $Y_k$ for $k\ge2$ — the Erlang is emphatically not memoryless (its density is zero at $0$, so a fresh Erlang wait and a partially elapsed one look different).
Example 3.4 (B&T Examples 6.10 and 6.11 — memorylessness at work).

(a) The tennis court. You arrive at a court and must wait for the current pair to finish; their total playing time is exponential. How long will you wait? By memorylessness, the remaining playing time has that same exponential PDF, no matter how long they have already been playing. The information "they started 40 minutes ago" is worthless.

(b) The three tellers. All three tellers are busy, no queue, and all service times are i.i.d. exponential. You start service the moment one finishes. What is the probability that you are the last of the three of you to leave? Focus on the instant your service begins. At that moment your own service time is fresh exponential; by memorylessness the two other customers' remaining times are also exponential with the same parameter. The three remaining times are therefore i.i.d., so by symmetry each is equally likely to be the largest: the answer is $1/3$. Note how the argument never computes an integral — memorylessness converts a messy "partially elapsed" situation into a symmetric one.

Practice 3.5. Typos appear in your writing as a Poisson process at $\lambda=12$ per hour. (a) What is the probability that you type for more than $10$ minutes without a typo? (b) What is the median time to the first typo?
Solution

(a) $T\sim\text{Exp}(12)$ with time in hours; $10$ min $=\tfrac16$ h. So $\mathbf{P}(T>\tfrac16)=e^{-12/6}=e^{-2}=0.135335$ (pq_33_P_gt_10min) — the same number as Practice 3.3(a), because both ask for "an empty window with $\lambda\tau=2$".

(b) Solve $F_T(m)=\tfrac12$: $1-e^{-\lambda m}=\tfrac12\Rightarrow e^{-\lambda m}=\tfrac12 \Rightarrow m=\ln 2/\lambda=0.693147/12=0.057762$ h $=3.465736$ minutes (pq_33_median_min). Note the median ($3.47$ min) is smaller than the mean ($60/12=5$ min): the exponential is right-skewed, so the mean is dragged out by the long tail.

3.4 The $k$th arrival time: the Erlang distribution

Let $Y_k$ be the time of the $k$th arrival, measured from time $0$. Since $Y_k=T_1+T_2+\cdots+T_k$ is a sum of $k$ i.i.d. exponential$(\lambda)$ gaps, its mean and variance come free from G2/G3's linearity and independence rules:

$$\mathbb{E}[Y_k]=\sum_{i=1}^k\mathbb{E}[T_i]=k\cdot\frac1\lambda=\frac k\lambda,\qquad \operatorname{var}(Y_k)=\sum_{i=1}^k\operatorname{var}(T_i)=k\cdot\frac1{\lambda^2}=\frac k{\lambda^2},$$

the variance step using independence of the $T_i$ (variances of independent random variables add; without independence we would need the covariance terms of G3 §4). The density needs real work, and L14 slide 6 states it without any [DERIVATION GAP]. Here are both standard derivations.

Derivation 1 — the small-interval argument (B&T §6.2, p. 317)

Fix $y>0$ and a tiny $\delta>0$. By the meaning of a density (G3 §1), $\delta\,f_{Y_k}(y)\approx\mathbf{P}(y\le Y_k\le y+\delta)$, with the approximation exact in the limit $\delta\to0$. Now, ignoring events of probability $o(\delta)$ (two arrivals in the same tiny slot), the $k$th arrival falls in $[y,y+\delta]$ exactly when both of the following happen:

  • $A$: there is an arrival during $[y,y+\delta]$ — probability $\mathbf{P}(A)\approx\lambda\delta$ by property 3;
  • $B$: there are exactly $k-1$ arrivals before time $y$ — probability $\mathbf{P}(B)=P(k-1,y)=\dfrac{(\lambda y)^{k-1}e^{-\lambda y}}{(k-1)!}$ by §3.2.

$A$ concerns $[y,y+\delta]$ and $B$ concerns $[0,y]$, disjoint intervals, so property 2 makes them independent and the probabilities multiply:

$$\delta f_{Y_k}(y)\approx\mathbf{P}(A\cap B)=\mathbf{P}(A)\mathbf{P}(B) \approx\lambda\delta\cdot\frac{(\lambda y)^{k-1}e^{-\lambda y}}{(k-1)!}.$$

Cancel the common factor $\delta$ and let $\delta\to0$:

$$\boxed{\ f_{Y_k}(y)=\frac{\lambda^k y^{k-1}e^{-\lambda y}}{(k-1)!},\qquad y\ge0\ }$$

where $\lambda\cdot(\lambda y)^{k-1}=\lambda^k y^{k-1}$. This is the Erlang density of order $k$, exactly as printed on L14 slide 6.

Derivation 2 — via the CDF, no approximations (B&T §6.2 footnote, p. 317)

The heuristic above is convincing but uses "$\approx$" twice. Here is the airtight version, and it also produces an identity you will use constantly. The key is another event identity: for $y\ge0$,

$$\{Y_k\le y\}=\{\text{at least }k\text{ arrivals in }[0,y]\}=\{N_y\ge k\},$$

since the $k$th arrival has already happened by time $y$ precisely when $[0,y]$ contains $k$ or more arrivals. Therefore

$$F_{Y_k}(y)=\mathbf{P}(N_y\ge k)=\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}P(n,y)=1-\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}P(n,y) =1-\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}\frac{(\lambda y)^n e^{-\lambda y}}{n!},$$

the middle step because the $P(n,y)$ over all $n\ge0$ sum to $1$. Differentiate term by term (a finite sum, so no convergence issues). Write $a_n(y)=\dfrac{(\lambda y)^ne^{-\lambda y}}{n!}$. By the product rule,

$$a_n'(y)=\frac{n\lambda(\lambda y)^{n-1}e^{-\lambda y}}{n!} -\frac{\lambda(\lambda y)^{n}e^{-\lambda y}}{n!} =\lambda\frac{(\lambda y)^{n-1}e^{-\lambda y}}{(n-1)!}-\lambda\frac{(\lambda y)^{n}e^{-\lambda y}}{n!} =\lambda a_{n-1}(y)-\lambda a_n(y),$$

where the first term used $\frac{d}{dy}(\lambda y)^n=n\lambda(\lambda y)^{n-1}$ and $n/n!=1/(n-1)!$, the second used $\frac{d}{dy}e^{-\lambda y}=-\lambda e^{-\lambda y}$, and we set $a_{-1}\equiv0$ so the $n=0$ case reads $a_0'=-\lambda a_0$, which is correct. Hence

$$f_{Y_k}(y)=F_{Y_k}'(y)=-\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}a_n'(y) =-\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}\big(\lambda a_{n-1}(y)-\lambda a_n(y)\big) =\lambda\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}\big(a_n(y)-a_{n-1}(y)\big)=\lambda a_{k-1}(y),$$

because the sum telescopes: every $a_n$ for $n\le k-2$ appears once with $+$ and once with $-$, and $a_{-1}=0$. Substituting $a_{k-1}(y)=\frac{(\lambda y)^{k-1}e^{-\lambda y}}{(k-1)!}$ gives $f_{Y_k}(y)=\lambda^k y^{k-1}e^{-\lambda y}/(k-1)!$ — the same Erlang density, with no approximation anywhere. As a numerical check, integrating the order-3 Erlang density with $\lambda=5$ from $0$ to $0.7$ gives $0.679153$, and $1-\sum_{n=0}^{2}P(n,0.7)$ gives $0.679153$; the difference is $0$ to machine precision (erl_cdf_lhs, erl_cdf_rhs, erl_cdf_dev). Each order-$k$ density also integrates to $1.000000$ (erl_norm_k1..3).

Property (the $k$th arrival time; L14 slide 6, B&T §6.2, p. 316). $$f_{Y_k}(y)=\frac{\lambda^ky^{k-1}e^{-\lambda y}}{(k-1)!}\ (y\ge0),\qquad \mathbb{E}[Y_k]=\frac k\lambda,\qquad \operatorname{var}(Y_k)=\frac k{\lambda^2},$$ and the tail identity $$\mathbf{P}(Y_k>y)=\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}P(n,y)=\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}\frac{(\lambda y)^ne^{-\lambda y}}{n!}.$$ For $k=1$ this is the exponential; the tail identity then reads $\mathbf{P}(Y_1>y)=e^{-\lambda y}$, as in §3.3.
Fig. 3.4 — The Erlang family, regenerated cleanly from the OCW figure on L14 slide 6 (which plots orders $r=1,2,3$; the shape parameter $r$ there is our $k$). Order $1$ is the exponential, with its maximum at $y=0$; every higher order starts at $0$, peaks at $(k-1)/\lambda$ and flattens as $k$ grows. Right: why — $Y_k$ is the sum of $k$ independent exponential gaps (L14 slide 6; B&T §6.2, p. 316).
Interpretation. Setting $f_{Y_k}'(y)=0$ gives the peak at $y=(k-1)/\lambda$ — one gap short of the mean $k/\lambda$. With $\lambda=5$ the peaks sit at $0,\,0.2,\,0.4$ for $k=1,2,3$ (erl_mode_k1..3), while the means are $0.2,\,0.4,\,0.6$. The density is right-skewed for every $k$, but the skew fades: since $Y_k$ is a sum of $k$ i.i.d. terms, the central limit theorem (G5) says it becomes bell-shaped, with mean $k/\lambda$ and standard deviation $\sqrt k/\lambda$. The relative spread $(\sqrt k/\lambda)/(k/\lambda)=1/\sqrt k$ again shrinks like $1/\sqrt k$.
Example 3.5 (B&T Example 6.12 — the IRS hotline). You call a hotline and are told you are the 56th person in line, excluding the person currently being served. Callers depart according to a Poisson process at rate $\lambda=2$ per minute. How long will you wait on average until your service starts, and what is the probability you wait more than 30 minutes?

Solution. Step 1 — model. Your service starts when the $56$th departure occurs: $55$ people ahead of you finish, and so does the one currently being served. By memorylessness the current customer's remaining service time is exponential$(2)$ just like a fresh one, so your wait is $Y=Y_{56}$, the $56$th arrival time of a Poisson process of rate $\lambda=2$ per minute: Erlang of order $k=56$.

Step 2 — the mean. $\mathbb{E}[Y]=k/\lambda=56/2=28$ minutes. Also $\operatorname{var}(Y)=k/\lambda^2=56/4=14$, so the standard deviation is $\sqrt{14}=3.741657$ minutes (irs_mean, irs_var, irs_sd).

Step 3 — the tail probability. B&T set up the integral $\mathbf{P}(Y\ge30)=\int_{30}^\infty \frac{\lambda^{56}y^{55}e^{-\lambda y}}{55!}dy$ and call it "quite tedious". Use the tail identity instead — no integration at all: $$\mathbf{P}(Y_{56}>30)=\sum_{n=0}^{55}P(n,30)=\sum_{n=0}^{55}\frac{60^ne^{-60}}{n!}=0.285491,$$ since $\lambda\cdot30=2\times30=60$ is the expected number of departures in 30 minutes. (In words: you are still waiting after 30 minutes exactly when fewer than 56 departures have occurred.) Independently evaluated with scipy.stats.erlang.sf the answer agrees to machine precision (irs_tail_exact, irs_tail_scipy, difference $0$).

Step 4 — the normal approximation B&T suggests. $Y$ is a sum of $56$ i.i.d. terms, so the central limit theorem applies: $$z=\frac{30-28}{\sqrt{14}}=\frac{2}{3.741657}=0.534522,\qquad \mathbf{P}(Y>30)\approx 1-\Phi(0.534522)=0.296490 .$$ That is off by $0.010999$ from the exact $0.285491$ (irs_z, irs_tail_clt, irs_clt_err) — about $3.9\%$ relative error, respectable for $k=56$ and a reminder that the Erlang is still slightly skewed at this order.

Practice 3.6. Photons hit a detector as a Poisson process with $\lambda=3$ per second. Let $Y_4$ be the arrival time of the fourth photon. Find $\mathbb{E}[Y_4]$, $\operatorname{var}(Y_4)$, and $\mathbf{P}(Y_4\le1)$.
Solution

$\mathbb{E}[Y_4]=k/\lambda=4/3=1.333333$ s and $\operatorname{var}(Y_4)=k/\lambda^2=4/9=0.444444$ (pq_34_mean, pq_34_var). For the probability, avoid the integral by using $\{Y_4\le1\}=\{N_1\ge4\}$ with $\lambda\tau=3\cdot1=3$: $$\mathbf{P}(Y_4\le1)=1-\sum_{n=0}^{3}\frac{3^ne^{-3}}{n!} =1-e^{-3}\Big(1+3+\tfrac92+\tfrac{27}{6}\Big)=1-13e^{-3}=0.352768 .$$ scipy.stats.erlang.cdf(1, 4, scale=1/3) returns the same $0.352768$, difference $0$ (pq_34_P_le_1, pq_34_scipy, pq_34_dev).

3.5 The Bernoulli/Poisson dictionary, and recitation 14

L14 slide 7 puts the two processes side by side. Everything in the table is a consequence of the single substitution $n=t/\delta$, $p=\lambda\delta$, $np=\lambda t$, followed by $\delta\to0$ — the annotation printed on the slide itself, and B&T §6.2, its Figure 6.6.

Fig. 3.5 — The Bernoulli/Poisson dictionary. The five rows of L14 slide 7 (times of arrival, arrival rate, PMF of the number of arrivals, interarrival time distribution, time to the $k$th arrival) are extended here with six further rows not on the slide: mean/variance of the count, mean interarrival time, mean time to the $k$th arrival, memorylessness, merging, and splitting — the last two previewed here and proved in §4 (L14 slide 7; B&T §6.2, its Figure 6.6).
Interpretation. Read a row left to right and you are taking the limit; read it right to left and you are discretizing. The two rows most worth internalizing are the third (binomial $\to$ Poisson: counts) and the fifth (geometric $\to$ exponential: waits). Notice how the mean/variance row degrades gracefully: $np(1-p)\to\lambda\tau$ precisely because $p=\lambda\delta\to0$, so the binomial's variance-deficit factor $(1-p)$ disappears and mean and variance coincide in the limit. And notice the merging row: Bernoulli merging needs the correction $-p_1p_2$ (both processes can succeed in the same slot), while Poisson merging is clean, $\lambda_1+\lambda_2$ — because simultaneous arrivals have probability zero.
Example 3.6 (L14 slide 8, [UNSOLVED EXAMPLE] — which process produced the next arrival?). A red bulb flashes as a Poisson process of rate $\lambda_1$, a green bulb independently as a Poisson process of rate $\lambda_2$. You record all flashes. What is the probability that the next flash is red?

Solution. Let $T_1\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda_1)$ be the time to the next red flash and $T_2\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda_2)$ the time to the next green one; they are independent by assumption. The next flash is red exactly when $T_1<T_2$. Condition on $T_1=t$ and use independence: $$\mathbf{P}(T_1<T_2)=\int_0^\infty f_{T_1}(t)\,\mathbf{P}(T_2>t)\,dt =\int_0^\infty \lambda_1e^{-\lambda_1 t}e^{-\lambda_2 t}\,dt =\lambda_1\int_0^\infty e^{-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)t}dt =\frac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_1+\lambda_2},$$ the last step because $\int_0^\infty e^{-ct}dt=1/c$ for $c>0$. With $\lambda_1=3$ and $\lambda_2=2$ the answer is $3/5=0.6$, and numerical integration of the same integral returns $0.600000$, difference $0$ (mrg_p_first, mrg_check_int). The merged process itself has rate $\lambda_1+\lambda_2=5$, since $\mathbf{P}(\min(T_1,T_2)>t)=e^{-\lambda_1t}e^{-\lambda_2t}=e^{-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)t}$. §4 develops merging and splitting properly — including the fact that the origins of successive merged arrivals are independent; this example is only the slide's teaser, answered.

Recipe (decision guide). See Fig. 3.6. Ask what kind of quantity the question is about — a count, a wait to the next arrival, or a wait to the $k$th arrival — and the distribution is forced. When a problem mixes the two languages ("what is the probability the third arrival comes after time $y$?"), convert with the identity $\{Y_k>y\}=\{N_y\le k-1\}$, which turns any waiting-time question into a counting question and usually removes an integral.
Fig. 3.6 — Which random variable does the question want? (L14 slides 4 and 6; B&T §6.2.)

Recitation 14, worked in full

Recitation 14 sits entirely on the Bernoulli side of the dictionary above: geometric waits, Pascal times, merging, random sums. Working it here is deliberate — every result you use has a Poisson twin one column to the right, and the contrast is the point.

Example 3.7 (rec14 P1 — mosquito and tick bites; Bernoulli processes and merging). You are visiting the rainforest and your insect repellent has run out. At each second, a mosquito lands on your neck with probability $0.5$; if one lands, with probability $0.2$ it bites you and with probability $0.8$ it never bothers you, independently of other mosquitoes. (a) What is the expected time between successive mosquito bites, and its variance? (b) In addition, at each second a tick lands with probability $0.1$; if one lands it bites with probability $0.7$, independently of everything else. Now what are the expected time and variance between successive bug bites?
Solution

(a) A "mosquito bite at second $i$" needs two independent things: a mosquito lands and it bites. So the per-second bite probability is $$p=\mathbf{P}(\text{lands})\cdot\mathbf{P}(\text{bites}\mid\text{lands})=0.5\times0.2=0.1$$ by the multiplication rule (G1 §2). Different seconds are independent and carry the same $p$, so mosquito bites form a Bernoulli process with parameter $p=0.1$ — this is the splitting row of Fig. 3.5, read on the Bernoulli side: thinning a Bernoulli$(0.5)$ process by keeping each arrival with probability $0.2$ gives Bernoulli$(0.5\times0.2)$.

Let $X$ be the time between successive bites — by memorylessness this is the same as the time until the next bite starting from now. It is geometric with parameter $p$, so from G2 §3 $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\frac1p=\frac1{0.1}=10\ \text{seconds},\qquad \operatorname{var}(X)=\frac{1-p}{p^2}=\frac{1-0.1}{0.1^2}=\frac{0.9}{0.01}=90 .$$ (r1_EX, r1_varX.) The standard deviation is $\sqrt{90}\approx9.5$ seconds — nearly as large as the mean, the geometric's signature.

(b) Tick bites form their own Bernoulli process, independent of the mosquitoes, with parameter $$q=0.1\times0.7=0.07 .$$ Bug bites are the merge of the two: a bug bite occurs in a second if a mosquito bites, or a tick bites, or both. By the inclusion–exclusion formula of G1 §1, $$r=\mathbf{P}(M\cup T)=\mathbf{P}(M)+\mathbf{P}(T)-\mathbf{P}(M\cap T)=p+q-pq,$$ where $\mathbf{P}(M\cap T)=pq=0.1\times0.07=0.007$ uses the independence of the two processes. Hence $$r=0.1+0.07-0.007=0.163 .$$ Equivalently and more memorably, $r=1-(1-p)(1-q)=1-0.9\times0.93=1-0.837=0.163$ — "not a bug bite" needs both processes to be quiet (r1_r, r1_r_alt, both $0.163$).

Merging preserves the Bernoulli structure (independent seconds, constant probability), so the time $Y$ between successive bug bites is geometric with parameter $r$: $$\mathbb{E}[Y]=\frac1r=\frac1{0.163}=6.134969\ \text{seconds},\qquad \operatorname{var}(Y)=\frac{1-r}{r^2}=\frac{0.837}{0.026569}=31.502879$$ (r1_EY, r1_varY; the official solution's $\approx6.135$ and $\approx31.503$). Sanity check: adding a second source of bites must shorten the wait, and $6.13<10$.

The Poisson twin. Had the problem been in continuous time with rates $\lambda_1$ and $\lambda_2$, the merged rate would be exactly $\lambda_1+\lambda_2$ with no $-pq$ correction, and the wait would be exponential with mean $1/(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)$. The correction term $-pq=-0.007$ is precisely the probability of a "collision" — both a mosquito and a tick biting in the same second — which is the discrete artifact that property 3 of §3.1 legislates away.

Example 3.8 (rec14 P2 — Al's and Bob's coin-flipping trials). Al performs a series of independent trials; on each trial he simultaneously flips three fair coins. (a) Given that Al has just had a trial with 3 tails, what is the probability that both of the next two trials also give 3 tails? (b) Al calls a trial a success whenever all three coins land the same side. (i) Find the PMF of $K$, the number of trials up to but not including the second success. (ii) Find the expectation and variance of $M$, the number of tails occurring before the first success. (c) Bob runs the same kind of experiment but starts with 4 coins, and whenever all his coins land the same side he permanently removes one coin and continues. He stops the third time he removes a coin. Find $\mathbb{E}[N]$, the number of trials in Bob's experiment.
Solution

(a) The trials are independent, so the conditioning information is irrelevant — this is the whole point of the question. For one trial, three fair coins give $2^3=8$ equally likely outcome strings, of which exactly one is TTT, so $\mathbf{P}(\text{3 tails})=\tfrac18$. Two specified consecutive trials both giving 3 tails has probability $$\Big(\tfrac18\Big)^2=\tfrac1{64}=0.015625$$ (r2a) by independence.

(b)(i) First the success probability, which the official solution never computes [SOLUTION GAP filled]: "all three the same" means HHH or TTT, two of the eight equally likely strings, so $$p_s=\mathbf{P}(\text{success})=\frac28=\frac14=0.25 .$$ Let $N$ be the trial on which the second success occurs. From §2.5 (L13 slide 6), $N$ is Pascal of order $2$: $$p_N(n)=\binom{n-1}{1}p_s^2(1-p_s)^{n-2},\qquad n=2,3,\dots$$ (choose which of the first $n-1$ trials held the first success, then demand success on trial $n$ and failure on the other $n-2$). The question asks for $K$ = trials up to but not including the second success, i.e. $K=N-1$. Substituting $n=k+1$: $$\boxed{\,p_K(k)=p_N(k+1)=\binom{k}{1}\Big(\frac14\Big)^2\Big(\frac34\Big)^{k-1} =k\cdot\frac1{16}\Big(\frac34\Big)^{k-1},\qquad k=1,2,3,\dots}$$ matching the recitation's answer. Note $p_K(0)=0$: the second success cannot arrive on trial 1. Checks: summing $p_K(k)$ numerically over $k$ gives $1.000000$, and $\mathbb{E}[K]=\mathbb{E}[N]-1=2/p_s-1=8-1=7$, confirmed numerically as $7.000000$ (r2_pK_check, r2_EK, r2_EK_formula).

(b)(ii) Write $M$ as a random sum (§2): $$M=X_1+X_2+\cdots+X_N,$$ where $X_i$ is the number of tails on the $i$th unsuccessful trial and $N$ is the number of unsuccessful trials before the first success. The $X_i$ are i.i.d. and independent of $N$ — knowing how many failures occurred says nothing about how the tails were arranged inside any one of them.

The distribution of $X$ [SOLUTION GAP filled]. Condition on the trial being a failure. Of the eight strings, the six failures are HHT, HTH, THH (one tail each) and HTT, THT, TTH (two tails each). All six are equally likely, so $X$ is uniform on $\{1,2\}$: $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\frac{1+2}2=\frac32,\qquad \mathbb{E}[X^2]=\frac{1^2+2^2}{2}=\frac52,\qquad \operatorname{var}(X)=\frac52-\Big(\frac32\Big)^2=\frac{10-9}{4}=\frac14 .$$

The distribution of $N$ [SOLUTION GAP filled]. Let $R$ be the trial number of the first success: $R$ is geometric with parameter $p_s=\tfrac14$, so $\mathbb{E}[R]=1/p_s=4$ and $\operatorname{var}(R)=(1-p_s)/p_s^2=(3/4)/(1/16)=12$. The number of failures is $N=R-1$, a shift, so $$\mathbb{E}[N]=4-1=3,\qquad \operatorname{var}(N)=\operatorname{var}(R)=12$$ (subtracting a constant moves a distribution but does not change its spread — G2 §3).

Apply the random-sum formulas of §2. $$\mathbb{E}[M]=\mathbb{E}[X]\,\mathbb{E}[N]=\frac32\times3=\frac92=4.5,$$ $$\operatorname{var}(M)=\mathbb{E}[N]\operatorname{var}(X)+\big(\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2\operatorname{var}(N) =3\times\frac14+\Big(\frac32\Big)^2\times12=\frac34+\frac94\times12=\frac34+27 .$$ [SOLUTION GAP filled — the recitation prints only the final fraction] Putting the two terms over a common denominator, $\frac34+27=\frac{3}{4}+\frac{108}{4}=\frac{111}{4}=27.75$ (r2_EM, r2_varM_term1, r2_varM_term2, r2_varM). Reading: almost all of the variance ($27$ of $27.75$) comes from not knowing how many failures there will be, not from the tails within a failure — the random-sum lesson of §2 in one number.

(c) Bob's experiment splits into three independent phases. Let $X$ be the number of trials until the first coin is removed (played with 4 coins), $Y$ the additional number until the second removal (3 coins), $Z$ the additional number until the third (2 coins); then $N=X+Y+Z$. [SOLUTION GAP filled] With $m$ fair coins there are $2^m$ equally likely strings and exactly two of them — all heads, all tails — are "all the same", so $$\mathbf{P}(\text{all }m\text{ same})=\frac{2}{2^m}=2^{-(m-1)}:\qquad m=4\Rightarrow\tfrac{2}{16}=\tfrac18,\quad m=3\Rightarrow\tfrac{2}{8}=\tfrac14,\quad m=2\Rightarrow\tfrac{2}{4}=\tfrac12 .$$ Each phase is a fresh sequence of independent trials with a constant success probability, so $X$, $Y$, $Z$ are geometric with parameters $\tfrac18$, $\tfrac14$, $\tfrac12$, and by linearity of expectation (no independence needed, though it holds): $$\mathbb{E}[N]=\mathbb{E}[X]+\mathbb{E}[Y]+\mathbb{E}[Z]=8+4+2=14$$ (r2c_EN). Bonus. Because the phases are independent, variances add: $\operatorname{var}(N)=\frac{7/8}{1/64}+\frac{3/4}{1/16}+\frac{1/2}{1/4}=56+12+2=70$ (r2c_varN) — a standard deviation of $\sqrt{70}\approx8.4$ trials, dominated entirely by the slow first phase.

Example 3.9 (rec14 P3 — signing $n$ papers drawn with replacement; the coupon collector). There are $n$ papers in a drawer. You draw a paper, sign it, and put it back. Every paper is equally likely on every draw, independently. What is the expected number of draws until every paper has been signed? (An answer as a summation is acceptable.)
Solution

Set-up. Let $M$ be the total number of draws until all $n$ papers are signed, and let $T_i$ be the number of draws made while exactly $i$ distinct papers are already signed, i.e. the number of draws needed to obtain the $(i+1)$st distinct paper. Then the draws partition into $n$ consecutive phases and $$M=T_0+T_1+\cdots+T_{n-1}.$$ $T_0=1$ always — the very first draw is necessarily new.

Each phase is geometric. Suppose $i$ papers are signed. A draw is a "success" if it lands on one of the $n-i$ unsigned papers, which happens with probability $$p_i=\frac{n-i}{n},$$ the same on every draw of the phase and independent of the others; so $\mathbf{P}(T_i=k)=(1-p_i)^{k-1}p_i$ for $k=1,2,\dots$, a geometric PMF.

Expected phase length [SOLUTION GAP filled]. The recitation jumps straight to $n/(n-i)$. The step is the geometric mean from G2 §3: $\mathbb{E}[T_i]=1/p_i$, hence $$\mathbb{E}[T_i]=\frac1{p_i}=\frac{n}{n-i}.$$

Add up. By linearity of expectation — which needs no independence, and is the reason this decomposition is so much easier than computing the distribution of $M$ — $$\mathbb{E}[M]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}T_i\Big]=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}[T_i] =\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{n}{n-i}.$$

Re-index [SOLUTION GAP filled]. Put $k=n-i$. As $i$ runs $0,1,\dots,n-1$, $k$ runs $n,n-1,\dots,1$ — the same set of values in reverse, and a finite sum does not care about order. So $$\boxed{\ \mathbb{E}[M]=n\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac1k=nH_n\ }$$ where $H_n$ is the $n$th harmonic number.

Asymptotics [SOLUTION GAP filled]. Since $1/x$ is decreasing, $\int_1^{n+1}\frac{dx}{x}\le H_n\le 1+\int_1^{n}\frac{dx}{x}$, i.e. $\ln(n+1)\le H_n\le 1+\ln n$: the sum is squeezed between two logarithms, so $H_n=\ln n+O(1)$ and $$\mathbb{E}[M]=nH_n\sim n\ln n .$$ More precisely $H_n=\ln n+\gamma+\frac1{2n}+O(n^{-2})$ with $\gamma=0.577216$ the Euler–Mascheroni constant.

Numbers (r3_*): for $n=10$, $H_{10}=2.928968$ so $\mathbb{E}[M]=29.289683$, against the crude $n\ln n=23.025851$ (ratio $1.272$) and the refined $n(\ln n+\gamma+\frac1{2n})=29.298008$ — three decimal places for free. A Monte-Carlo simulation of $200{,}000$ runs at $n=10$ averages $29.289280$, confirming the formula (r3_sim_n10). The ratio $nH_n/(n\ln n)$ falls to $1.126$ at $n=100$ and $1.084$ at $n=1000$: the $\gamma$ term matters for a long time.

Interpretation. The last phase alone costs $\mathbb{E}[T_{n-1}]=n$ draws — chasing the final missing paper takes as long as all $n$ papers' worth of drawing. That single term is why the answer is $n\ln n$ and not $n$.

Practice 3.7. Three independent Bernoulli processes of bites operate on you each second: mosquitoes with $p_1=0.1$, ticks with $p_2=0.07$, and now horseflies with $p_3=0.05$. Find the parameter of the merged process, the expected time between bug bites, and its variance.
Solution

"No bite this second" requires all three to miss, and they are independent, so $$1-r=(1-p_1)(1-p_2)(1-p_3)=0.9\times0.93\times0.95=0.79515,\qquad r=0.20485$$ (exactly $\tfrac{4097}{20000}$; pq_35_merged). The complement form is far cleaner than three-way inclusion–exclusion. The gap is geometric with parameter $r$: $$\mathbb{E}=\frac1r=4.881621\ \text{s},\qquad \operatorname{var}=\frac{1-r}{r^2}=\frac{0.79515}{0.041964}=18.948600$$ (pq_35_E, pq_35_var). Compare with Poisson: if instead the three were Poisson processes with rates $0.1,0.07,0.05$ per second, the merged rate would be exactly $0.22$ and the mean gap $1/0.22=4.545$ s — shorter, because the Bernoulli version wastes the seconds in which two species bite simultaneously.

Waiting times, disambiguated

Example 3.9 answered the coupon collector in general. Because the six-sided die is the instance everybody meets first — and the one exam questions usually mean — it is worth writing out with the numbers substituted; and because three other classic questions are phrased almost identically and have different answers, the recipe after it separates them.

Example 3.10 (the coupon collector on a fair die — Example 3.9 with $n=6$). You roll a fair six-sided die repeatedly. Let $M$ be the number of rolls until every one of the six faces has appeared at least once. Find $\mathbb{E}[M]$ and $\operatorname{var}(M)$.

Solution. Step 1 — set up the phases. This is Example 3.9 verbatim with the $n$ papers replaced by the $6$ faces: each roll picks a face uniformly at random, independently, with replacement. Let $T_i$ be the number of rolls made while exactly $i$ distinct faces have already been seen — that is, the rolls needed to produce the $(i+1)$st new face. The rolls split into six consecutive phases, so $$M=T_0+T_1+T_2+T_3+T_4+T_5 .$$

Step 2 — each phase is geometric, with its own $p$. While $i$ faces are already in hand, a roll is a "success" if it shows one of the $6-i$ faces not yet seen. All six faces are equally likely, so by the discrete uniform probability law (G1 §1), $$p_i=\frac{6-i}{6},$$ the same on every roll of the phase and independent of the other rolls; hence $T_i$ is geometric with parameter $p_i$ and, by the geometric mean of G2 §3, $\mathbb{E}[T_i]=1/p_i=6/(6-i)$. Stage by stage (die_p0..5, die_ET0..5):

faces already seen $i$$0$$1$$2$$3$$4$$5$
success probability $p_i=\frac{6-i}6$$1$$\frac56$$\frac46=\frac23$$\frac36=\frac12$$\frac26=\frac13$$\frac16$
expected wait $\mathbb{E}[T_i]=\frac6{6-i}$$1$$1.2$$1.5$$2$$3$$6$

Step 3 — add them up. By linearity of expectation, which needs no independence: $$\mathbb{E}[M]=\sum_{i=0}^{5}\frac{6}{6-i} =\frac66+\frac65+\frac64+\frac63+\frac62+\frac61 =6\Big(1+\tfrac12+\tfrac13+\tfrac14+\tfrac15+\tfrac16\Big)=6H_6 ,$$ the middle equality being the re-indexing $k=6-i$ of Example 3.9. Since $H_6=\tfrac{49}{20}=2.45$ exactly, $$\boxed{\ \mathbb{E}[M]=6\times\tfrac{49}{20}=\tfrac{147}{10}=14.7\ \text{rolls}\ }$$ (die_H6, die_EM).

Step 4 — the variance. Unlike the mean, this one does need independence, and it is available: the phases are determined by disjoint blocks of rolls, so $T_0,\dots,T_5$ are independent and variances add (G2 §3 for the geometric variance $\operatorname{var}(T_i)=(1-p_i)/p_i^2$): $$\operatorname{var}(M)=\sum_{i=0}^{5}\frac{1-p_i}{p_i^2} =0+\frac{1/6}{(5/6)^2}+\frac{1/3}{(2/3)^2}+\frac{1/2}{(1/2)^2}+\frac{2/3}{(1/3)^2}+\frac{5/6}{(1/6)^2},$$ i.e. $0+0.24+0.75+2+6+30=\tfrac{3899}{100}=38.99$, so the standard deviation is $\sqrt{38.99}=6.244197$ rolls (die_varM, die_sdM). The general closed form $\operatorname{var}(M)=n^2\sum_{k=1}^n k^{-2}-nH_n$ returns the same $\tfrac{3899}{100}$, difference $0$ (die_varM_closedform, die_varM_dev).

Step 5 — Monte Carlo. Simulating $400{,}000$ runs of the die gives a mean of $14.701437$ against the exact $14.7$ — a gap of $0.001437$, well inside the simulation's standard error $\sqrt{38.99/400000}=0.009873$ — and a sample variance of $38.896288$ against $38.99$ (die_sim_EM, die_sim_dev, die_sim_se, die_sim_varM).

Reading the answer. Two features are worth carrying away. First, the last face costs $6$ rolls on its own — nearly half the total budget of $14.7$ goes on chasing one remaining face, exactly the effect that turns $n$ into $n\ln n$. Second, the spread is enormous: $14.7\pm6.2$, and the distribution is right-skewed, so "about fifteen rolls" is a statement about the average and not about your next attempt. (The crude asymptotic $n\ln n=6\ln6=10.750557$ is $27\%$ low at $n=6$; the harmonic sum, not its logarithm, is the answer to quote — die_nlogn, die_ratio.)

Recipe (the waiting-time zoo — four exam questions that sound alike). Every one of these asks "how many trials until …", and the four answers are different. Read the question for what stops the clock, then use the matching row. Numbers are for a fair coin, $p=\tfrac12$ (verified as zoo_*).
The question stops at…Model$\mathbb{E}[\text{trials}]$Fair-coin valueDerived in
the first success ("first head")geometric$(p)$$\dfrac1p$$2$G2 §3
the $k$th success, anywhere ("$2$ heads total")Pascal / negative binomial of order $k$$\dfrac kp$$4$§2.5
a pattern ("$2$ heads in a row", HH)first-step analysis on a small state chain$\dfrac{1+p}{p^2}$ for HH$6$G2 §3.8; general machinery G5 §4
every category collected ("all $6$ faces")coupon collector: sum of $n$ geometrics$nH_n=n\sum_{k=1}^n\frac1k$$14.7$ for $n=6$Examples 3.9–3.10
  1. "Total" or "in a row"? This is the distinction that decides rows 2 and 3, and it is the one that gets missed. Two heads total takes $k/p=4$ tosses on average; two heads in a row takes $6$.
  2. Why the pattern costs more. A run can be broken. In the Pascal problem every head is banked forever, so the waits for the two heads simply add: $2+2=4$. In the HH problem a tail after a lone head throws that head away and returns you to the start — progress is destroyed, not just paused. The extra $\mathbb{E}[\text{HH}]-\mathbb{E}[\text{2 heads total}]=6-4=2$ tosses are the price of those restarts (zoo_gap).
  3. "All of them" means coupon collector, not "$n$ times as long as one of them". The phases have different success probabilities $(n-i)/n$, so you add $n/(n-i)$, not $n\cdot n$.
  4. Every row is a sum of geometrics except row 3. Rows 1, 2 and 4 all decompose into independent geometric waits and are finished by linearity; row 3 cannot, because the state can go backwards — which is precisely when you draw the state diagram and write one equation per state.
Gotcha. "Expected number of tries before you succeed" and "expected number of tries until you succeed" differ by exactly $1$: the geometric $X$ counts the successful trial, so $\mathbb{E}[X]=1/p$, while the number of failures $X-1$ has mean $1/p-1=(1-p)/p$. The same $\pm1$ trap sits in Example 3.8(b)(i) above ($K=N-1$). Decide which one the question wants before you quote a number, and say so in your answer.
Practice 3.8 (the surprising pair). A fair coin is tossed repeatedly. Find the expected number of tosses until the pattern HTH first appears, and until HHH first appears. Both patterns have probability $\tfrac18$ in any three specified tosses — do they take the same time?
Solution

No, and that is the point. Use the row-3 method: a state records the length of the longest suffix of the tosses so far that is a prefix of the target pattern; $E_s$ is the expected number of further tosses from state $s$; the target state has $E=0$; and each state satisfies $E_s=1+\sum_{s'}\mathbf{P}(s\to s')E_{s'}$, the $1$ counting the toss you are about to make (G2 §3.8).

HHH. States $S_0,S_1,S_2$ = number of trailing heads banked. From $S_0$: heads (probability $\tfrac12$) $\to S_1$, tails $\to S_0$. From $S_1$: heads $\to S_2$, tails $\to S_0$ (the tail wipes out the run). From $S_2$: heads $\to$ done, tails $\to S_0$. So $$E_0=1+\tfrac12E_1+\tfrac12E_0,\qquad E_1=1+\tfrac12E_2+\tfrac12E_0,\qquad E_2=1+\tfrac12\cdot0+\tfrac12E_0 .$$ Substituting the third into the second, $E_1=1+\tfrac12(1+\tfrac12E_0)+\tfrac12E_0=\tfrac32+\tfrac34E_0$; substituting that into the first, $E_0=1+\tfrac12(\tfrac32+\tfrac34E_0)+\tfrac12E_0 =\tfrac74+\tfrac78E_0$, so $\tfrac18E_0=\tfrac74$ and $\mathbb{E}=14$. This is the $k=3$ case of the formula $(1+p+\cdots+p^{k-1})/p^k$ of G2 §3.8 (pq_38_HHH).

HTH. States $S_0$ (nothing useful), $S_1$ (last toss H), $S_2$ (last two tosses HT). From $S_0$: H $\to S_1$, T $\to S_0$. From $S_1$: H $\to S_1$ (a second head is still a single useful trailing H, not a reset), T $\to S_2$. From $S_2$: H $\to$ done, T $\to S_0$. So $$E_0=1+\tfrac12E_1+\tfrac12E_0,\qquad E_1=1+\tfrac12E_1+\tfrac12E_2,\qquad E_2=1+\tfrac12\cdot0+\tfrac12E_0 .$$ From the first, $E_0=2+E_1$. From the third, $E_2=1+\tfrac12E_0=1+\tfrac12(2+E_1)=2+\tfrac12E_1$. Putting that in the second, $E_1=1+\tfrac12E_1+\tfrac12(2+\tfrac12E_1)=2+\tfrac34E_1$, so $\tfrac14E_1=2$, $E_1=8$ and $\mathbb{E}=E_0=2+8=10$ (pq_38_HTH).

Why HTH is faster by $4$ tosses (pq_38_diff). Compare what a failure costs. In HHH, one tail at any point destroys all banked progress and sends you to $S_0$. In HTH, the failure in state $S_1$ is a head — but a head is itself the start of a fresh HTH, so you stay in $S_1$ and lose nothing at all. Patterns that overlap themselves badly (HHH agrees with a shift of itself in two places) wait longer than patterns that recover cheaply. Monte Carlo over $300{,}000$ runs each: $13.988203$ for HHH and $9.991020$ for HTH (pat_sim_HHH, pat_sim_HTH). For the record, HHT and THH both take $8$ (pat_E_HHT, pat_E_THH) — the answer depends on the pattern, not just on its length or its probability.

Practice 3.9 (one fair die, four questions). Roll a fair six-sided die repeatedly. Find the expected number of rolls until (a) the first $6$; (b) the third $6$; (c) two $6$s in a row; (d) all six faces have appeared. Identify which row of the zoo each one is.
Solution

Throughout, $p=\mathbf{P}(\text{a specified face})=\tfrac16$ and $1-p=\tfrac56$.

(a) Row 1, geometric. $\mathbb{E}=1/p=6$ rolls (pq_39_first_six).

(b) Row 2, Pascal of order $3$. The waits for the first, second and third $6$ are i.i.d. geometric, so $\mathbb{E}=k/p=3\times6=18$ rolls (pq_39_three_sixes).

(c) Row 3, first-step analysis. With $\mathbb{E}[N]=(1+p)/p^2$ from G2 §3.8, $$\mathbb{E}[N]=\frac{1+\tfrac16}{(\tfrac16)^2}=\frac{7/6}{1/36}=\frac{7\times36}{6}=42$$ rolls (pq_39_two_in_row). Note $42\ne 2\times6=12$ and $42>18$: with a low-$p$ trial, a broken run is catastrophic, so the in-a-row question is far harder than the total-count question — the same gap as $6$ versus $4$ for the coin, but hugely amplified.

(d) Row 4, coupon collector. $\mathbb{E}[M]=6H_6=14.7$ rolls, from Example 3.10 (pq_39_all_faces).

The point. One die, four English sentences that a careless reader would call the same question, four answers: $6$, $18$, $14.7$, $42$. Notice also that (d) is smaller than (b): collecting all six faces is easier than waiting for three copies of one prescribed face, because in (d) every early roll makes progress toward some missing face.

Practice 3.10 (progress already made). You are rolling the fair die for all six faces. (a) You have seen five distinct faces so far. How many more rolls do you expect to need, and does it matter how many rolls you have already spent? (b) What is the expected number of rolls to get from $0$ to $3$ distinct faces, and from $3$ distinct faces to all $6$?
Solution

(a) The remaining wait is the single phase $T_5$, geometric with $p_5=\tfrac16$, so $\mathbb{E}[T_5]=6$ additional rolls (pq_310_last_face). It does not matter how long you have been rolling: the geometric is memoryless (G2 §3), so the $30$ rolls you may already have wasted on repeats change nothing about the future. This is the discrete twin of the memorylessness of §3.3, and it is why one stubborn face can eat the whole budget.

(b) The first three faces cost $$\mathbb{E}[T_0]+\mathbb{E}[T_1]+\mathbb{E}[T_2]=\frac66+\frac65+\frac64=1+1.2+1.5=3.7$$ rolls, and the last three cost $$\mathbb{E}[T_3]+\mathbb{E}[T_4]+\mathbb{E}[T_5]=\frac63+\frac62+\frac61=2+3+6=11$$ rolls (pq_310_first_three, pq_310_remaining), and indeed $3.7+11=14.7$ as it must. Reading: the first half of the collection takes a quarter of the time and the second half takes three quarters. Collecting is cheap while most categories are still missing and expensive at the end — the shape of every coupon-collector problem.

§3 cheatsheet — the Poisson process.
  • Definition (L14 slide 3). Time homogeneity ($P(k,\tau)$ depends only on $\tau$), independence across disjoint intervals, and small-interval probabilities $P(0,\delta)=1-\lambda\delta+o(\delta)$, $P(1,\delta)=\lambda\delta+o_1(\delta)$, $P(k\ge2,\delta)=o_k(\delta)$. One parameter: the rate $\lambda$, in arrivals per unit time.
  • Counts. $N_\tau\sim$ Poisson$(\lambda\tau)$: $P(k,\tau)=(\lambda\tau)^ke^{-\lambda\tau}/k!$, $\mathbb{E}=\operatorname{var}=\lambda\tau$. Derived as the $n\to\infty$ limit of binomial$(n,\lambda t/n)$ with $np=\lambda t$ pinned. Only the total length of the time set matters.
  • Waits. $T\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda)$: $\mathbf{P}(T>t)=e^{-\lambda t}$, $f_T(t)=\lambda e^{-\lambda t}$, $\mathbb{E}=1/\lambda$, $\operatorname{var}=1/\lambda^2$. Interarrival times are i.i.d. exponential and memoryless: $\mathbf{P}(T>t+s\mid T>t)=\mathbf{P}(T>s)$.
  • $k$th arrival. $Y_k\sim\text{Erlang}(k,\lambda)$: $f_{Y_k}(y)=\lambda^ky^{k-1}e^{-\lambda y}/(k-1)!$, $\mathbb{E}=k/\lambda$, $\operatorname{var}=k/\lambda^2$, peak at $(k-1)/\lambda$.
  • The bridge identity. $\{Y_k>y\}=\{N_y\le k-1\}$, so $\mathbf{P}(Y_k>y)=\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}(\lambda y)^ne^{-\lambda y}/n!$ — this converts every waiting-time question into a counting question and kills the Erlang integral.
  • Dictionary. binomial$\to$Poisson, geometric$\to$exponential, Pascal$\to$Erlang, via $n=t/\delta$, $p=\lambda\delta$, $np=\lambda t$, $\delta\to0$. Bernoulli merging is $p_1+p_2-p_1p_2$; Poisson merging is $\lambda_1+\lambda_2$, with the next arrival coming from process 1 with probability $\lambda_1/(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)$ (developed in §4).
  • The waiting-time zoo (fair coin, $p=\tfrac12$). First success $\to$ geometric, $1/p=2$ (G2 §3). The $k$th success total $\to$ Pascal, $k/p=4$ for $k=2$ (§2.5). A pattern such as HH $\to$ first-step analysis, $(1+p)/p^2=6$ (G2 §3.8; G5 §4 for the general machinery); HTH takes $10$ and HHH takes $14$. All $n$ categories $\to$ coupon collector, $nH_n$; for a fair die $6H_6=14.7$ rolls with $\operatorname{var}=38.99$ (Example 3.10). A broken run discards progress; a banked success never does — that is the whole $6$-versus-$4$ story.
  • rec14 numbers. P1: $p=0.1\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}[X]=10$, $\operatorname{var}=90$; $r=0.163\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}[Y]=6.134969$, $\operatorname{var}=31.502879$. P2: $\tfrac1{64}$; $p_K(k)=k(\tfrac14)^2(\tfrac34)^{k-1}$; $\mathbb{E}[M]=\tfrac92$, $\operatorname{var}(M)=\tfrac{111}4$; $\mathbb{E}[N]=14$. P3: $\mathbb{E}[M]=nH_n\sim n\ln n$ ($29.289683$ at $n=10$).

§4   Poisson II: merging, splitting, and random incidence

Sources: L15 slides 1–8 (raster p01–p02) · rec15 problems 1–3 · Bertsekas–Tsitsiklis §6.2 (splitting and merging of Poisson processes, Examples 6.13–6.16; the random incidence paradox and its Figure 6.7; B&T Problems 6.14 and 6.15). Every number on this page is recomputed in computes/g4_s4.py (checked against closed forms, numerical integration, and Monte Carlo); every figure is generated by computes/g4_s4_figs.py.

Section 3 built the Poisson process from three defining properties — time homogeneity, independence over disjoint intervals, and the small-interval probabilities $\mathbf{P}(1,\delta)\approx\lambda\delta$, $\mathbf{P}(0,\delta)\approx1-\lambda\delta$ — and read off its three distributions: the count $N_\tau\sim\text{Poisson}(\lambda\tau)$, the interarrival time $T\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda)$, and the time to the $k$th arrival $Y_k\sim\text{Erlang}(k)$. This section is about what happens when you combine or take apart such processes, and about one genuinely startling consequence of doing so.

Three operations, then one paradox. Merging: superpose two independent Poisson streams and the result is Poisson at the sum of the rates — which immediately answers "whose arrival comes first?" and turns a hard three-exponential integral (rec15 P3) into two fractions. Splitting: label each arrival by an independent coin flip and each label class is itself Poisson — and, far less obviously, the two label classes are independent processes. Random incidence: an observer who shows up at an arbitrary instant and measures the gap he lands in does not see a typical gap; he sees one with twice the mean length. That last result explains why the bus always seems late, and it is the formal version of the bus paradox you met informally in G2.

Interpretation. Merging and splitting are the continuous-time twins of the Bernoulli operations in §2: merging two Bernoulli processes ORs the trial indicators, splitting thins a Bernoulli process by a coin. Every proof below is the same two-line argument — check the three defining properties on an interval of infinitesimal length $\delta$, discarding anything of order $\delta^2$ — so once you have seen it once you have seen it three times.

4.1 Warm-up: Poisson fishing (the review toolkit at work)

Before combining processes, L15 opens (slide 3) with a single-process problem that uses every tool from §3 at once. The slide poses six questions and answers none of them, so we do all six. Every quantity below is a fresh computation in computes/g4_s4.py.

Example 4.1 (Poisson fishing; L15 slide 3, all six parts — the slide leaves every answer blank). Fish arrive at your hook as a Poisson process of rate $\lambda=0.6$ per hour. Your policy: fish for two hours; if you have caught nothing by then, keep fishing until your first catch, and stop the moment it happens. Find (a) $\mathbf{P}(\text{fish for more than two hours})$; (b) $\mathbf{P}(\text{fish for more than two and less than five hours})$; (c) $\mathbf{P}(\text{catch at least two fish})$; (d) $\mathbb{E}[\text{number of fish}]$; (e) $\mathbb{E}[\text{future fishing time}\mid\text{fished for four hours}]$; (f) $\mathbb{E}[\text{total fishing time}]$.

Setting up the notation. Let $N_\tau$ be the number of fish caught in an interval of length $\tau$; by §3, $N_\tau\sim\text{Poisson}(\lambda\tau)$, so $$\mathbf{P}(k,\tau)=\mathbf{P}(N_\tau=k)=\frac{(\lambda\tau)^ke^{-\lambda\tau}}{k!},\qquad k=0,1,2,\ldots$$ Let $T$ be the total time you spend fishing (a random variable, because the policy is state-dependent) and let $N$ be the total number of fish you catch. With $\lambda=0.6$ and a two-hour block, the relevant Poisson parameter is $\lambda\cdot2=1.2$ (fish_lam_t2).

(a) The policy says: you fish beyond two hours exactly when the first two hours produced no catch. These are the same event, so $$\mathbf{P}(T>2)=\mathbf{P}(N_2=0)=\frac{(1.2)^0e^{-1.2}}{0!}=e^{-1.2}=0.301194 .$$ (fish_a_P_more_than_2.) About $30\%$ of trips run long.

(b) The event $\{2<T<5\}$ needs two things: no catch in $[0,2]$ (otherwise $T=2$), and then a catch somewhere in $(2,5)$ (otherwise $T\ge5$). Those two statements concern the disjoint intervals $[0,2]$ and $(2,5)$, whose length is $2$ and $3$, so by the independence property they multiply: $$\mathbf{P}(2<T<5)=\mathbf{P}(N_2=0)\cdot\mathbf{P}(N_{3}\ge1) =e^{-1.2}\left(1-e^{-0.6\cdot3}\right)=0.301194\cdot\left(1-0.165299\right)$$ $$=0.301194\cdot0.834701=0.251407 .$$ (fish_exp_m18 $=e^{-1.8}=0.165299$, fish_b_between_2_and_5.)

(c) Here is the subtlety the slide hides. If you catch at least one fish in the first two hours, you stop at time $2$ having caught $N_2$ fish. If you catch none, you continue to your first catch and then stop — with exactly one fish. So the total catch is $$N=\max(N_2,1),$$ and "at least two fish" can only happen through the first branch: $\{N\ge2\}=\{N_2\ge2\}$. Therefore $$\mathbf{P}(N\ge2)=1-\mathbf{P}(N_2=0)-\mathbf{P}(N_2=1) =1-e^{-1.2}-1.2\,e^{-1.2}=1-2.2\,e^{-1.2},$$ using $\mathbf{P}(N_2=1)=(1.2)^1e^{-1.2}/1!=1.2e^{-1.2}=0.361433$. Numerically $$\mathbf{P}(N\ge2)=1-0.301194-0.361433=1-0.662627=0.337373$$ (fish_c_at_least_two; a $400{,}000$-trip simulation gives $0.338210$, fish_mc_c_at_least_two).

(d) Write the total catch as $N=\max(N_2,1)=N_2+\mathbf{1}_{\{N_2=0\}}$: the two expressions agree when $N_2=0$ (both give $1$) and when $N_2\ge1$ (both give $N_2$). Expectation is linear, and the expectation of an indicator is the probability of its event (G2 §2), so $$\mathbb{E}[N]=\mathbb{E}[N_2]+\mathbf{P}(N_2=0)=\lambda\cdot2+e^{-1.2}=1.2+0.301194=1.501194$$ (fish_d_E_num_fish; simulation $1.502065$). Note it exceeds $\mathbb{E}[N_2]=1.2$: the "keep going" clause adds the missing fish on the unlucky trips.

(e) You are told you have fished for four hours. Under the policy that means no catch in $[0,4]$. By the fresh-start property, the process after time $4$ is a Poisson process with the same rate $\lambda$, independent of the past, so the additional waiting time until the first catch is $\text{Exp}(\lambda)$ regardless of the four barren hours: $$\mathbb{E}[\text{future fishing time}\mid\text{fished 4 hours}]=\frac1\lambda=\frac1{0.6} =1.666667\ \text{hours}=100\ \text{minutes}$$ (fish_e_future_time). This is memorylessness: the fish have no memory of your bad afternoon.

(f) Condition on the first two hours and use the total expectation theorem (§1): $$\mathbb{E}[T]=\mathbf{P}(N_2\ge1)\cdot\mathbb{E}[T\mid N_2\ge1]+\mathbf{P}(N_2=0)\cdot\mathbb{E}[T\mid N_2=0].$$ On the first branch $T=2$ exactly, so $\mathbb{E}[T\mid N_2\ge1]=2$. On the second branch you have already spent $2$ hours and, by memorylessness again, expect $1/\lambda$ more, so $\mathbb{E}[T\mid N_2=0]=2+1/\lambda$. Substituting, with $q=\mathbf{P}(N_2=0)=e^{-1.2}$, $$\mathbb{E}[T]=(1-q)\cdot2+q\left(2+\frac1\lambda\right)=2+\frac{q}{\lambda} =2+\frac{0.301194}{0.6}=2+0.501990=2.501990\ \text{hours}$$ (fish_f_E_total_time; simulation $2.501243$, fish_mc_f_E_total_time). The "$2+$" is the guaranteed block; the "$q/\lambda$" is the expected overtime.

Gotcha. In part (c) it is tempting to write $\mathbf{P}(N\ge2)=1-\mathbf{P}(N=0)-\mathbf{P}(N=1)$ and then to hunt for $\mathbf{P}(N=0)$. But $\mathbf{P}(N=0)=0$: the policy guarantees you never leave empty-handed. Whenever the stopping rule depends on what has happened so far, write the random variable of interest explicitly as a function of the underlying counts — here $N=\max(N_2,1)$ — before computing anything.
Practice 4.1. Same fishing policy, $\lambda=0.6$/hour. (i) What is $\mathbf{P}(\text{you catch exactly one fish})$? (ii) Given that you fish for more than two hours, what is your expected total fishing time?
Solution

(i) Since $N=\max(N_2,1)$, the event $\{N=1\}$ occurs exactly when $N_2=0$ (you catch one fish after the two-hour mark) or $N_2=1$. These are disjoint, so $$\mathbf{P}(N=1)=\mathbf{P}(N_2=0)+\mathbf{P}(N_2=1)=e^{-1.2}+1.2e^{-1.2}=2.2e^{-1.2} =0.301194+0.361433=0.662627$$ (fish_pr_P_exactly_one_fish). Two-thirds of trips end with exactly one fish — as a check, $0.662627+0.337373=1$ together with part (c), and $\mathbf{P}(N=0)=0$.

(ii) Given $T>2$ (equivalently $N_2=0$), memorylessness restarts the process at time $2$, so the remaining time is $\text{Exp}(0.6)$ with mean $1/0.6$: $$\mathbb{E}[T\mid T>2]=2+\frac1{0.6}=2+1.666667=3.666667\ \text{hours}$$ (fish_pr_E_T_given_more_than_2). Compare with the unconditional $\mathbb{E}[T]=2.501990$: knowing the first two hours failed costs you more than an hour of expected extra fishing.

4.2 Merging independent Poisson processes

Two independent Poisson processes are running: a red bulb flashing at rate $\lambda_1$ and a green bulb flashing at rate $\lambda_2$ (L15 slide 4). Record all flashes on one time axis and forget the colors. What kind of process do you get?

Fig. 4.1 — Merging: the merged process holds the union of the two mark sets, and its rate is the sum (L15 slide 4; B&T §6.2, Example 6.14).
Property (merging; L15 slide 4, B&T §6.2, Example 6.14). Let the red process have rate $\lambda_1$ and the green process rate $\lambda_2$, and let the two processes be independent of each other. Then the merged process — an arrival is recorded whenever either process has one — is Poisson with rate $\lambda_1+\lambda_2$. Moreover, each merged arrival comes from the red process with probability $\lambda_1/(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)$, independently of all other arrivals and of the arrival times.

Derivation (checking the three defining properties on a small interval). Let $\delta>0$ be small and consider the interval $(t,t+\delta]$; "negligible" below always means "of order $\delta^2$ or smaller", which vanishes relative to $\delta$ as $\delta\to0$.

  1. Time homogeneity. $\lambda_1$ and $\lambda_2$ are constants, so the probability law of the merged count in an interval depends on the interval's length only, not on where it sits. ✓
  2. Independence over disjoint intervals. Take two disjoint intervals $I$ and $J$. The red counts on $I$ and $J$ are independent (property of the red process); the same for green; and the red process is independent of the green process by hypothesis. The merged count on $I$ is a function of (red on $I$, green on $I$) and the merged count on $J$ is a function of (red on $J$, green on $J$); these two pairs involve four mutually independent random variables, so functions of them are independent (G2 §4). ✓
  3. Small-interval probabilities. For no merged arrival, both processes must be quiet: $$\mathbf{P}(0\text{ merged arrivals in }\delta)=(1-\lambda_1\delta)(1-\lambda_2\delta) =1-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)\delta+\lambda_1\lambda_2\delta^2\approx1-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)\delta,$$ the product step using independence of the two processes and the last step dropping the $\delta^2$ term. For exactly one merged arrival, either red fires and green does not, or the other way round — disjoint cases, so add: $$\mathbf{P}(1\text{ merged arrival in }\delta)=\lambda_1\delta(1-\lambda_2\delta) +(1-\lambda_1\delta)\lambda_2\delta=(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)\delta-2\lambda_1\lambda_2\delta^2 \approx(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)\delta .$$ Two or more merged arrivals requires at least two arrivals somewhere, whose probability is of order $\delta^2$: negligible. ✓

These are exactly the defining properties of a Poisson process of rate $\lambda_1+\lambda_2$, so the merged process is Poisson with that rate. Consequently the merged count over an interval of length $\tau$ is $\text{Poisson}\big((\lambda_1+\lambda_2)\tau\big)$ and the merged interarrival times are $\text{Exp}(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)$.

Whose arrival is it? Competing exponentials

L15 slide 4 asks: what is the probability that the next arrival comes from the first process? — and gives no answer. Here it is, twice.

First derivation (small intervals; the lecturer's argument). Condition on the merged process having exactly one arrival in $(t,t+\delta]$, and ask for the probability it is red. By the definition of conditional probability, $$\mathbf{P}(\text{red}\mid1\text{ merged arrival}) =\frac{\mathbf{P}(\text{red arrival and no green arrival})}{\mathbf{P}(1\text{ merged arrival})} \approx\frac{\lambda_1\delta}{(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)\delta}=\frac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_1+\lambda_2},$$ where numerator and denominator come from step 3 above and the $\delta$'s cancel — so the answer does not depend on $\delta$ and survives the limit $\delta\to0$. Because distinct arrivals occur in disjoint (infinitesimal) intervals, and a Poisson process treats disjoint intervals independently, the labels of successive merged arrivals are independent Bernoulli trials with success probability $\lambda_1/(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)$ (B&T §6.2, Example 6.14).

Second derivation (direct integration). Let $T_1\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda_1)$ and $T_2\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda_2)$ be the first arrival times, independent. Then, conditioning on the value of $T_1$ and using the joint density $f_{T_1,T_2}(t_1,t_2)=f_{T_1}(t_1)f_{T_2}(t_2)$ from G3 §2, $$\mathbf{P}(T_1<T_2)=\int_0^\infty f_{T_1}(t)\,\mathbf{P}(T_2>t)\,dt =\int_0^\infty \lambda_1e^{-\lambda_1t}\,e^{-\lambda_2t}\,dt =\lambda_1\int_0^\infty e^{-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)t}\,dt$$ $$=\lambda_1\left[\frac{-e^{-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)t}}{\lambda_1+\lambda_2}\right]_0^\infty =\lambda_1\left(0+\frac1{\lambda_1+\lambda_2}\right)=\frac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_1+\lambda_2}.$$ With $\lambda_1=2$ and $\lambda_2=3$ this is $2/5=0.400000$; numerical quadrature returns $0.400000$ (merge_int_p_first_from_1) and a $400{,}000$-sample simulation returns $0.399985$ (merge_mc_p_first_from_1).

Property (competing exponentials; B&T §6.2, Example 6.15). If $T_1,\dots,T_n$ are independent with $T_i\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda_i)$, then $$Z=\min\{T_1,\dots,T_n\}\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda_1+\cdots+\lambda_n),\qquad \mathbf{P}(T_i=Z)=\frac{\lambda_i}{\lambda_1+\cdots+\lambda_n},$$ and the winner's identity is independent of the winning time.

Derivation of the minimum. For $z\ge0$, the event $\{Z>z\}$ means every $T_i$ exceeds $z$; independence turns the intersection into a product: $$\mathbf{P}(Z>z)=\mathbf{P}(T_1>z,\dots,T_n>z)=\prod_{i=1}^n\mathbf{P}(T_i>z) =\prod_{i=1}^ne^{-\lambda_iz}=e^{-(\lambda_1+\cdots+\lambda_n)z},$$ which is the exponential tail with parameter $\sum_i\lambda_i$; hence $F_Z(z)=1-e^{-(\sum\lambda_i)z}$ and $Z\sim\text{Exp}(\sum_i\lambda_i)$. This fills the gap flagged in the rec15 P3 solution, where "$\min\{Y,Z\}$ is exponential with rate $\mu+\nu$" is asserted without proof. For $\lambda_1=2,\lambda_2=3$: $\mathbb{E}[Z]=1/5=0.200000$, simulation $0.199639$ (merge_mc_E_min).

Interpretation. Merging says the same thing twice. Read it forward: put two streams on one axis, rates add. Read it backward: a race between independent exponential clocks is settled by a single exponential clock at the summed rate, and the winner is chosen by a rate-weighted coin flip that carries no information about when the race ended. That second reading is what makes long chains of exponential races (light bulbs, queues, Markov chains in G5) tractable.
Gotcha. Merging needs the component processes to be independent. Two Poisson processes that always fire together (say, green copies red) merge into a Poisson process of rate $\lambda$, not $2\lambda$. And the merged process is Poisson only if each component is; merging two arbitrary renewal processes gives something that is only approximately Poisson (B&T §6.2 discusses why superposing many small independent streams — telephone calls in a city — is nonetheless well modeled as Poisson).

The light bulb example

Fig. 4.2 — The three-bulb race as three merged races in sequence (L15 slide 5; B&T §6.2, Example 6.16).
Example 4.2 (three light bulbs; L15 slide 5 — the slide states the problem and gives no solution; B&T Example 6.16). Each light bulb has an independent $\text{Exp}(\lambda)$ lifetime. You install three at time $0$. Find the expected time until the last bulb dies out, and its variance.

Solution. Let $T_{\text{last}}$ be the time until the last of the three dies; equivalently $T_{\text{last}}=\max\{X_1,X_2,X_3\}$ where $X_i\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda)$ is the $i$th bulb's lifetime. Attacking the maximum directly means differentiating $F_{\max}(x)=\left(1-e^{-\lambda x}\right)^3$; the merging view is easier and more instructive. Think of each bulb's death as the first arrival of its own Poisson process of rate $\lambda$; the three processes are independent because the lifetimes are.

Stage 1 (three bulbs alive). The first death is the first arrival of the merged process of rate $\lambda+\lambda+\lambda=3\lambda$, so the time $T_1$ until it is $\text{Exp}(3\lambda)$ and $$\mathbb{E}[T_1]=\frac1{3\lambda}.$$ Stage 2 (two bulbs alive). At the instant of the first death, memorylessness says the two surviving bulbs' remaining lifetimes are again independent $\text{Exp}(\lambda)$ random variables, independent of everything before. Merging those two, the additional time $T_2$ to the second death is $\text{Exp}(2\lambda)$: $$\mathbb{E}[T_2]=\frac1{2\lambda}.$$ Stage 3 (one bulb alive). Memorylessness once more: the last bulb's remaining lifetime $T_3$ is $\text{Exp}(\lambda)$, so $\mathbb{E}[T_3]=1/\lambda$.

The total time is $T_{\text{last}}=T_1+T_2+T_3$ and expectation is linear: $$\mathbb{E}[T_{\text{last}}]=\frac1{3\lambda}+\frac1{2\lambda}+\frac1\lambda =\frac{2+3+6}{6\lambda}=\frac{11}{6\lambda}.$$ For $\lambda=1$ that is $0.333333+0.500000+1.000000=1.833333$ (bulb_E_total); a $400{,}000$-sample simulation of $\max\{X_1,X_2,X_3\}$ gives $1.832153$ (bulb_mc_E_total).

Variance. The three stage-times are independent — that is exactly what memorylessness buys: each stage restarts the relevant clocks from scratch. So variances add (G2 §3), and $\operatorname{var}(\text{Exp}(\mu))=1/\mu^2$: $$\operatorname{var}(T_{\text{last}})=\frac1{(3\lambda)^2}+\frac1{(2\lambda)^2}+\frac1{\lambda^2} =\frac1{9\lambda^2}+\frac1{4\lambda^2}+\frac1{\lambda^2}=\frac{4+9+36}{36\lambda^2} =\frac{49}{36\lambda^2}.$$ For $\lambda=1$: $1.361111$ (bulb_var_total), simulation $1.356234$.

Gotcha. $\mathbb{E}[\max]\ne\max$ of the means, and the three stage durations are not the three lifetimes. $T_2$ is not "the second bulb's lifetime"; it is the extra time after the first death. Adding the three lifetimes would give $3/\lambda$, which is not $11/(6\lambda)$.
